### [1] [Collaborative In-Context Learning for Indoor Radio Map Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31702)
*Yuanzhe Peng, Jie Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Indoor radio map estimation (RME) is essential for wireless applications such as coverage planning, localization, and network deployment. However, most existing methods follow a supervised learning paradigm that requires costly labeled data and task-specific model training. Moreover, indoor environments involve privacy-sensitive information, such as building layouts and structures, that often cannot be centralized. To address these challenges, we make the first attempt to formulate indoor RME as a collaborative in-context learning (ICL) problem. We propose Poly-Ripple, a framework in which multiple clients perform ICL using large autoregressive vision models (LAVMs) without model updates. Each client is prompted by its private demonstrations and predicts the radio map for the same scene query. To capture the causal structure of wireless propagation outward from the transmitter (Tx), we adopt ripple-ordered autoregressive modeling. We further introduce importance-aware aggregation to exploit heterogeneous contextual knowledge distributed across clients under the context length constraint. We provide theoretical analysis and conduct extensive experiments on a public indoor RME benchmark. The experimental results show that Poly-Ripple consistently outperforms representative ICL baselines, while aligning with our theoretical analysis.


### [2] [End-to-End Differentiable Diffusion Conditioning for Physics-Informed Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31721)
*Ricardo Luna Gutierrez, Vineet Gundecha, Rahman Ejaz, Varchas Gopalaswamy, Riccardo Betti, Sahand Ghorbanpour, Aarne Lees, Soumyendu Sarkar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Optimizing high-dimensional physical systems under expensive evaluations and complex constraints is a central challenge in science and engineering. In offline model-based optimization (MBO), methods must improve designs using only a fixed dataset and no test-time access to the true objective. However, existing methods typically suffer from conservatism, unconstrained surrogate exploitation, or function as static inference-time samplers that cannot refine candidates against precise physical objectives. In this paper, we propose Differentiable Diffusion Conditioning (D2C) for offline MBO, which leverages the fact that deterministic sampling makes the reverse denoising chain differentiable with respect to the conditioning signal. D2C turns a frozen conditional diffusion model into an end-to-end gradient-based optimizer by directly optimizing conditioning variables at test time via gradients from frozen surrogate objectives and physics-informed penalties backpropagated through the reverse chain. We implement this idea in a composed proposer-evaluator framework and evaluate it on laser pulse optimization for inertial confinement fusion, sustainable data-center workload scheduling, and four Design-Bench tasks. Our method achieves the strongest results on the two real-world tasks and the best average rank across four Design-Bench tasks.


### [3] [Autoregressive Multimodal Anomaly Detection with Large Language Models for Microservice Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31722)
*Yixiang Tang, Fagui Liu, Guoxiang Zhong, Xi Yao, C.L. Philip Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Microservice systems have become a dominant architecture for large-scale cloud applications, enabling rapid deployment and elastic scaling. However, ensuring the reliability and availability of microservice systems critically relies on effective anomaly detection, as failures and performance anomalies can quickly propagate across services and severely impact end-to-end system behavior. Existing multimodal anomaly detection approaches for microservice systems often struggle with two fundamental limitations: (i) the neglect of modality-specific characteristics in favor of cross-modality fusion, and (ii) the "identity-shortcut" problem that affects the feature reconstruction process. To address these issues, we propose LLMMAD, an autoregressive multimodal anomaly detection framework for microservice systems based on Large Language Models (LLMs). Initially, to reconcile cross-modality fusion with modality-specific preservation, LLMMAD embeds and recombines multimodal data into a unified sequence for LLM processing to model inter-modality relationships, while employing modality-wise reconstruction heads to retain modality-specific information. Subsequently, to eliminate the identity-shortcut, we implement a prediction-driven autoregressive objective, under which the model predicts future states rather than copies current inputs. Finally, we conduct extensive experiments on three real-world datasets. The results demonstrate that LLMMAD consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving F1-score improvements of 3.1%, 1.4%, and 0.9%, respectively, thereby providing a more reliable solution for microservice monitoring.


### [4] [Learning to Be Monitored: Causal Bounds and Intervention-Aware Post-Training Recipes for Chain-of-Thought Monitorability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31733)
*Qiang Fu, Xinyue Li, Andre Wibisono, Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring aims to detect potentially harmful behaviors in large language models (LLMs) from their CoTs. Intervention-based evaluations measure this property by asking whether a monitor can detect the role of an intervention from the CoT when the targeted behavior occurs, typically using minimal-criterion sensitivity ($\mathsf{TPR_{mc}}$) proposed by~\citep{guan2025monitoring}. However, a target behavior observed under intervention is not necessarily caused by it, and we show that this metric can overestimate how often a monitor detects target behaviors actually caused by the intervention. Specifically, we establish $\mathsf{TPR_{mc}}$ as an upper bound on this causal detection rate and derive a complementary lower bound, which provides a tighter evaluation framework for CoT monitorability. With this framework, we study whether LLMs can be trained to make their CoTs easier to monitor. We first construct a training dataset of paired control--intervention tasks spanning multiple intervention types. Using the dataset, we design and apply three intervention-specific training methods based on off-policy self-distillation, reinforcement learning, and on-policy self-distillation to train separate variants of each base model. Across various model-monitor configurations, all three methods generally improve CoT monitorability over the corresponding base models under our extended evaluation framework. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [5] [Benchmarks Are Surprisingly Compaction-Friendly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31778)
*Jeanne Salle, Hardik Bhatnagar, Matthias Bethge, Sahar Abdelnabi, Maksym Andriushchenko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Long horizon agentic tasks are now pushing LLM agents past their context window. To continue the task, the agent scaffold has to free up the context window, a process known as context compaction. Compaction is generally lossy by design: agents can silently lose task constraints, instructions, or their reasoning behind earlier decisions. Although compaction is a standard component of coding agent scaffolds, it has not been studied systematically with frontier models on realistic, long horizon agentic tasks. Previous work evaluate smaller open weight models on toy or synthetic trajectories. We fix the agent scaffold (OpenCode), implement five compaction strategies inside it, and evaluate three frontier models on three long horizon benchmarks (PaperBench, ProgramBench, PostTrainBench). For the two 1M context models, the compaction threshold does not measurably affect the task performance, whereas GPT-5.3-Codex, the model with the smallest context window, performs worse at lower thresholds on PaperBench and, less clearly, on PostTrainBench. No compaction method outperforms default self-summarization, and methods that discard more content cost some models up to 20 points while leaving other models the same. Compaction can, however, silently remove task constraints: on ProgramBench, the first compaction summary already drops about one third of the task's anticheating rules on average, and this rises monotonically with the number of compactions. On real user-agent sessions from SWE-chat, instructions about how the agent should work and what it should not do are dropped around 6 times more than specifications of what to build. We argue that current benchmarks are compaction-friendly: i.e., the objective is fixed and the agent's progress stays on disk, so most of what compaction discards can be recovered, except constraints that don't exist anywhere else.


### [6] [Not All Retained Clients Are Equal: Client Selection for Federated Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31786)
*Thanh Linh Nguyen, Quoc-Viet Pham*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Federated unlearning (FU) aims to remove a designated target client's influence from a trained model without the prohibitive cost of retraining from scratch. Existing methods largely treat unlearning as a post-hoc, stateless operation, overlooking that client influence is path-dependent: contributions made during training, particularly during critical learning periods (CLPs), can have disproportionate and persistent effects. Moreover, knowledge overlap and intermittent client availability make relying on all retained clients inefficient and impractical. We introduce PDF, a path-dependent client-selection framework based on the insight that retained clients are not equally valuable for unlearning. During training, PDF maintains lightweight, target-agnostic, CLP-weighted histories summarizing each client's accumulated update direction and global-model alignment. Upon an unlearning request, these histories identify and rank retained clients that are globally informative yet weakly coupled to the target, enabling retraining-based unlearning with only the top-$k$ clients over a small number of rounds. Experiments on FMNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 under IID and non-IID settings show that PDF achieves effective forgetting and strong retained utility with substantially fewer retained clients. In particular, PDF achieves $0\%$ forgetting accuracy with retraining-like MIA AUC on non-IID CIFAR-10, improves retained accuracy by up to $6.91$ percentage points over the strongest competing FU baseline on CIFAR-100, and reduces communication by approximately $69\%$ and computation by $57$--$61\%$ relative to Retrain.


### [7] [Data Quality Audit and Bivariate Temperature-Humidity Analysis of a Long-Term LoRaWAN Network: Anomaly Detection, PCA, Clustering and Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32438)
*Mamadou Sow*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Long-term environmental IoT/LPWAN deployments often aggregate heterogeneous data streams from diverse devices without systematic auditing of each variable's actual coverage prior to analysis-an increased risk when multiple sensor types coexist within the same network. This work proposes a C++17 processing pipeline applied to the Tour Perret LoRaWAN dataset (421 937 frames, June 2021--June 2023, 5 devices). A systematic coverage audit integrated into the extraction phase reveals that CO2 is completely missing (0% coverage) and pressure is reported only by an isolated base module (Wyres, 4.24% of the corpus), invalidating the initial hypothesis of a joint four-variable analysis. The analysis is refocused on the Temperature--Humidity pair, jointly available across 205{,}005 frames (95.76%) from 4 Elsys EMS sensors. The pipeline sequentially performs anomaly detection (modified Z-score, Isolation Forest), bivariate Principal Component Analysis, clustering (K-means, DBSCAN), and a multilayer perceptron predicting humidity from temperature and time, whose residuals serve as a detector for slow drift. On real-world data: Temperature--Humidity correlation r=-0.662, two-component PCA (PC1 =83.1% of variance), Isolation Forest detects 2.12% multivariate anomalies while univariate Z-score detects none, calibrated DBSCAN isolates 0.17% noise across 13 clusters, and the MLP achieves $R^2=0.672$ (RMSE =10.54%) in cross-validation. This work demonstrates that per-variable coverage auditing prior to modeling is a necessary methodological step for heterogeneous multi-sensor IoT datasets.


### [8] [The Path to NDE 4.0: A Review of Algorithms, Techniques, and Strategies for Autonomous Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31704)
*Ashiqur Rahmana, Venkata Devesh Reddy Seethi, Zachary Kral, Austin Yunker, Rajkumar Kettimuthu, Hamed Alhoori*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Non-Destructive Inspection (NDI) is a cornerstone of modern quality assurance, critical for ensuring the safety and reliability of infrastructure and manufactured components without causing damage. This paper presents a comprehensive review of the current NDI landscape, analyzing the field through four distinct lenses, namely, computational algorithms, physical techniques, industrial applications, and research objectives. We synthesize a broad range of academic literature to illustrate the rapid evolution from manual, analog interpretation to a digitized, data-centric paradigm known as NDE 4.0. Our analysis highlights the dominance of ultrasound and visual inspection as primary physical techniques, while revealing a significant shift toward deep learning and generative models for automated defect recognition. Despite these technological strides, the industry faces critical bottlenecks, notably the scarcity of standardized open-access datasets and the "black box" nature of AI models, which hinders widespread adoption. By charting these developments and identifying key research gaps, such as the need for explainable AI (XAI) and standardized digital twin protocols, this review offers a strategic roadmap for future research. It concludes that the future of NDI research should focus on the successful integration of intelligent, autonomous fusion of inspection data with advanced sensor technologies into asset lifecycle management.


### [9] [Does LeWorldModel Preserve Local Action Geometry?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31716)
*Vasil Vasilev, Siddharth Nayak*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We examine how closely pretrained LeWorldModel (LeWM) reproduces the effects of small action changes. Using matched simulator and model responses, we measure action sensitivity with Gram matrices at three perturbation scales. Across nearly a thousand states from Reacher and OGBench Cube, the most sensitive action directions differ by median angles of about $36^\circ$ and $42^\circ$, respectively, at the smallest scale. Increasing the perturbation tenfold reduces median normalized Gram discrepancy by roughly 70\% on Reacher and 47\% on Cube. Geometry measured in pixels and embeddings varies more across scales than the chosen physical-state geometry. On held-out directions, environment Grams achieve median embedding response errors of 17\% and 28\%, compared with 26\% and 38\% for an isotropic reference with the same trace. LeWM's errors are higher at this scale. These findings support scale-specific evaluation of action sensitivity; its effect on control remains an open question.


### [10] [Towards Adversarially Robust Multigraph Neural Networks for Financial Crime Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31718)
*Dennis Heijmans, H. Çağrı Bilgi, Rui Wang, Kubilay Atasu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Financial transaction networks are naturally represented as multigraphs, where repeated transactions between the same pair of accounts are modeled as distinct edges. Recently proposed multigraph neural network architectures leverage this structure explicitly to achieve a strong performance on anti-money laundering (AML) tasks. However, their robustness to adversaries that manipulate transaction structures to evade detection remains largely unexplored. We present the first scalable framework for structural attacks and adversarial training of multigraph neural networks using node-edge incidence matrices, adapting projected gradient descent and projected randomized block coordinate descent methods to optimize structural perturbations over such matrices. We further introduce a cycle-based regularizer that penalizes changes in the global counts of directed 2- and 3-cycles, providing a structural measure of attack plausibility. Experiments on synthetic pattern-detection and realistic AML datasets show that our learned attacks substantially outperform non-learnable perturbation baselines in reducing detection performance (PR-AUC), while adversarial training consistently improves robustness against these attacks. These results demonstrate that multigraph neural networks are vulnerable to learned structural attacks and that adversarial training provides an effective defense. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [11] [Cognitively Constrained Partners for Zero-Shot Coordination in Real-Time Partitioned Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31726)
*Ash Simmons, George De Ath, Richard M. Everson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Effective human-machine teaming requires agents that coordinate with unpredictable human partners. Reinforcement learning from live human feedback or large behavioural datasets is expensive, while purely simulated training overfits to artificial conventions and fails at Zero-Shot Coordination with real people. We propose a framework for training teaming agents against Cognitively Constrained Partners: simulated partners whose diversity emerges from procedural constraints grounded in human physiological literature, specifically reaction delays and perceptual limitations, rather than from reward shaping or historical training checkpoints. In a modified Lunar Lander environment, we report preliminary evidence that these partners play more like real people than unconstrained agents do, and that agents trained against them match the strongest cross-play baselines while being the only ones whose performance does not depend on how their training partners were generated. We also describe a human-participant study designed so that its rankings can be compared directly against cross-play, testing how well that standard evaluation predicts real human-agent teaming.


### [12] [When and How Does Muon Precondition? The Role of Structured Gradient Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31728)
*Xiaoran Chen, Binghui Li, Liwei Wang, Pinyan Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Muon has become a popular optimizer for large language model training, yet its theoretical foundations remain poorly understood. In this work, we study how gradient noise geometry shapes Muon's preconditioning benefits. We first show, theoretically and empirically, that under the classical isotropic noise assumption, Muon offers no acceleration beyond a rescaling of the learning rate. This motivates us to investigate structured noise arising from mini-batch sampling in associative memory with heterogeneous task frequencies. We establish an exact balancing threshold for two tasks and extend the analysis to multiple tasks, revealing how task coverage and gradient reliability jointly shape learning. Our analysis shows that Muon can soften the dependence on task frequency from linear to square-root, helping balance the learning of frequent and rare tasks. We further identify rotation-induced negative transfer in SignSGD that Muon avoids, and explain how momentum reduces sampling noise while tracking changing gradients. Controlled numerical experiments validate these mechanisms, and a preliminary LLM pretraining study illustrates the practical promise of fast-slow momentum.


### [13] [ConsMSA: Semantic Distribution Consistency Learning for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31731)
*Pan Wang, Lipeng Ke, Huajun Ying, Pritish Mohapatra, Rohan Sarkar, Suresh Lakhani, Sankar Venkataraman, Jingtong Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multimodal sentiment analysis (MSA) aims to predict human sentiments by integrating signals from different modalities such as text, video, and audio. However, raw multimodal sequences often suffer from semantic inconsistency--exhibiting redundancy or conflicts within and across modalities--which hinders robust understanding and increases computational cost. To this end, we introduce ConsMSA, which explicitly formalizes semantic distribution consistency across both $\textit{intra}$- and $\textit{inter}$-modality, providing a consistency-aware mechanism for robust and efficient multimodal sentiment prediction. Specifically, ConsMSA projects multimodal token features into a shared sentiment space to compute an Intra- and Inter-modality Consistency Score ($I^2CS$). By coupling this score with predictive relevance, we formulate consistency-aware importance signals that are utilized: (i) as a consistency regularizer to align latent distributions during training, (ii) to derive semantic-aware weights for adaptive multimodal token reweighting, and (iii) as a practical criterion to prune redundant or conflicting tokens. Extensive experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and CH-SIMS demonstrate that ConsMSA achieves state-of-the-art performance while remaining robust under aggressive token compression--retaining only 10% of tokens yields comparable accuracy. These results establish semantic distribution consistency as a promising foundation for synergizing predictive robustness with computational efficiency.


### [14] [Efficient Omni-modal Large Language Model Inference via Per-Query Modality Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31768)
*Shuqi Liu, Yuzhou Cao, Luke Ong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) can jointly process a broad range of modalities, such as text, audio, and video, enabling stronger multimodal understanding and reasoning capabilities, but often at substantial inference cost. We observe that the benefit of additional modalities varies across queries, indicating that a fixed modality configuration can be inefficient. Motivated by this, we study query-wise modality selection, which keeps the underlying Omni-LLM fixed while adaptively selecting which modalities to provide for each query. We formulate the problem as an accuracy-cost optimization problem and develop a cost-sensitive learning approach for modality selection. We evaluate our approach on WorldSense using Qwen2.5-Omni and Qwen3-Omni under three inference-cost measures: input tokens, latency, and monetary cost. Across both models, query-wise modality selection consistently improves the accuracy-cost trade-off and can even outperform the standard setting in which all available modalities are provided to the Omni-LLM. For Qwen2.5-Omni, our cost-sensitive approach matches or exceeds this full-modality accuracy while requiring only approximately 41% of the input tokens, 39% of the latency, and 61% of the monetary cost.


### [15] [PaperScorer: How Predictable Is Paper Acceptance?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31791)
*Sachin Konan, Jonathan Liu, Zhuang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Automated research is largely evaluated by benchmark performance, but the value of research rarely reduces to a single score. Humans judge a work's holistic contribution through peer review, which is hard to model because it is noisy and depends on many factors. However, what if, given only the paper, we could predict its peer-review decision directly? We introduce PaperScorer, a family of models that predict a paper's accept or reject decision from its extracted text or page screenshots, training only on the decision after removing author identity and formatting shortcuts. Vision models are the most predictive (68% accuracy on ICLR papers and 77% on ML papers from arXiv), beating text-only and frontier reasoning models. Unlike plain text, page images retain figures, layout, and presentation. This signal is robust, surviving smaller models and even compressing the whole paper into a single image. But accurate predictions are not evenly spread. PaperScorer is strongest on the lowest and highest rated papers, while papers rated in the middle stay unpredictable for our models and frontier systems alike. Additionally, its confidence is calibrated, so its most confident predictions are also its most accurate. Overall, PaperScorer shows how much of the decision a paper carries on its own, marking a first step toward AI-driven research evaluation.


### [16] [CuraLoc: Repository-Level Fault Localization via Planned Code Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31809)
*Sangeeta Warrier, Anush Sankaran, Ryen White*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A bug report describes what went wrong, but rarely where the code should change. Existing repository-level localization methods often let language models iteratively decide what code to inspect next, requiring repeated model calls and substantial token usage. We investigate whether these inspection decisions can instead be planned before source retrieval. In this research, we introduce CuraLoc, which first selects candidate files and represents their symbols using compact behavioral and structural descriptions. In a single planning decision, the model jointly selects starting symbols, chooses code relationships to inspect from each, and forms hypotheses about likely edits. Deterministic retrieval collects the requested source for a final ranking informed by those hypotheses, concentrating model reasoning on choosing and comparing code rather than directing each retrieval step. We demonstrate the efficacy of our proposed approach on the SWE-Bench Lite and Verified datasets. On SWE Bench Verified with GPT-5.2, CuraLoc raises Func@1 from LocAgent's 53.8% to 59.3% while using 90.3% fewer recorded tokens, and also outperforms RepoSearcher (56.2%) and Agentless (39.6%) on Func@1. On SWE-bench Lite, CuraLoc achieves a high 83.8% Func@5 accuracy improving on Agentless by more than 15% in relative accuracy. Additionally, CuraLoc supports different model backbones, with strong localization accuracy demonstrated using both GPT-5.2 and GPT-6 Astra. These results provide evidence that repository-level code inspection can be planned effectively without iterative model-controlled navigation.


### [17] [Nested Sparsity for Scalable Pretraining and Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31818)
*Tianzhe Chu, Druv Pai, Yi Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) transformers, the standard backbone for modern large-scale deep learning, employ conditional computation: only a sparse subset of model weights is used in each forward pass. In MoE transformers, this sparsity arises in the MoE operator, which generalizes the multi-layer perceptron in the classical transformer. In this work, we explore an alternative mechanism for conditional computation that analogously generalizes the attention operator. The sparsity induced by this mechanism naturally nests (i.e., hierarchically composes) with the sparsity induced by the MoE operator. We show that a transformer employing this nested sparsity mechanism, which we call Nestor, performs better than a conventional MoE transformer at equal compute and equal sparsity in language model pretraining. We also show that Nestor is well-suited for continual pretraining: it can be adapted to longer contexts more effectively than conventional MoEs, as well as adapt more efficiently to new domains while forgetting less on old domains. Furthermore, we demonstrate that these benefits persist as model size, dataset size, and context length grow. Our results demonstrate the potential of alternative conditional computation mechanisms for deep neural network architectures. Code and checkpoints are available on GitHub at this https URL .


### [18] [Speculative Diffusion is a Tale of Two Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31819)
*Aaron Zorel, Guy Van den Broeck, Aditya Grover, Daniel Israel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Speculative decoding is a technique that accelerates the inference speed of autoregressive large language models (LLMs) by sampling from a faster, lower quality draft model. Traditionally, a fundamental characteristic of speculative decoding is that it is lossless with respect to the verifier distribution, guaranteeing speed without sacrificing quality. This differs from recently developed diffusion LLMs (dLLMs), which are notably able to achieve substantial speedups by degrading the quality of their outputs with less diffusion effort. Because diffusion models are able to sample faster than autoregressive models by generating tokens in parallel, they are considered strong candidates for draft models in speculative diffusion. While it is widely understood that diffusion models can sacrifice quality for speed, speculative decoding itself can also be generalized to generate lossy samples faster using more lenient acceptance criteria with respect to the verifier distribution. Presented with the two parameters of diffusion effort and speculative decoding leniency, this paper explores the full achievable Pareto frontier via joint variations of these parameters. Our experiments show that jointly varying diffusion effort and acceptance leniency can extend the speed-quality Pareto frontier over single step diffusion drafting.


### [19] [Rethinking Multi-View Fusion for Urban Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31850)
*Sean Bin Yang, Zongyi Xu, Jilin Hu, Ying Sun, Kristian Torp, Bin Yang, Torben Bach Pedersen, Christian S. Jensen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Urban region representation learning encodes heterogeneous urban signals, such as mobility flows, points of interest, and land-use patterns, into unified embeddings for downstream tasks, including check-in prediction, crime forecasting, and service demand estimation. Existing approaches rely on globally shared fusion and joint optimization, implicitly assuming uniform modality importance and naturally balanced multi-objective training. However, urban data is inherently heterogeneous. Our empirical analysis reveals two structural issues: (i) modality utility varies markedly across regions, causing static fusion to overemphasize signals from certain modalities; and (ii) heterogeneous objectives exhibit persistent gradient imbalance, causing representational bias and unstable training. We present PRISM, a self-adjusting framework for adaptive multi-view learning. PRISM adapts sparse MoE routing to learn region-specific view allocations, while a PID (Proportional Integral Derivative)-inspired regulation mechanism mitigates gradient conflicts during optimization. Experiments on three large-scale urban datasets show that PRISM achieves competitive performance compared with strong multi-view baselines, while providing more stable optimization behavior and more balanced region representations.


### [20] [Cautious Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31855)
*Damian Hattler, Qiang Liu, Nils Thuerey*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Partial differential equations (PDEs) are indispensable tools for modeling complex phenomena in physics and engineering. Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) have emerged as a powerful alternative to traditional numerical solvers by directly incorporating physical constraints into neural network loss functions. Despite their advantages, PINN training requires multi-objective optimization, which often leads to significant gradient conflicts among boundary, initial, and residual loss terms. The Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient (ConFIG) method addresses these conflicts; however, integrating ConFIG with adaptive optimizers such as Adam introduces a critical vulnerability: the conflict-free direction generated by ConFIG is further processed by the optimizer, and the resulting weight update may no longer be conflict-free with respect to the task gradients. To address this issue, Cautious ConFIG is proposed as a concise three-line extension to the ConFIG algorithm that empirically restores the conflict-freeness guarantee prior to the weight update. The method is evaluated on three PINN benchmarks: the 1D Viscous Burgers' equation, the 1D Nonlinear Schrödinger equation, and the 2D Kovasznay flow. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements over the standard ConFIG baseline. Additionally, the stabilizing effect of cautious masking and its reaction to conflict severity, task number, and optimizer lag are investigated using a simple toy system.


### [21] [Aligning Large Language Models with Human Feedback: Mathematical Foundations and Algorithm Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31862)
*Siliang Zeng, Luca Viano, Chenliang Li, Jiaxiang Li, Volkan Cevher, Markus Wulfmeier, Stefano Ermon, Alfredo Garcia, Mingyi Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This article provides an introduction to the mathematical foundations and algorithmic frameworks used to align Large Language Models (LLMs) with human intentions, preferences, and values. We discuss standard alignment techniques, such as fine-tuning (SFT), reinforcement learning with human feedback (RLHF), and direct preference optimization (DPO). We also explore the theoretical underpinnings of learning from human preferences, drawing connections to inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) and discrete choice models. We present state-of-the-art algorithms in a tutorial style, discuss their advantages and limitations, and offer insights into practical implementation. Our exposition is intended to serve as a comprehensive resource for researchers and practitioners, providing both a foundational understanding of alignment methodologies and a framework for developing more robust and scalable alignment techniques.


### [22] [Rewriting Scaling Laws for Synthetic Data with Compute and Data Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31867)
*Nicholas Roberts, Ahmed Ahmed, Suhas Kotha, Percy Liang, Yu Wang, Alex Renda, Nitya Mani*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Synthetic rewrites, where a language model rewrites a natural document e.g. into a target style, are now a standard ingredient of pretraining corpora. However, critical aspects of their use remain poorly understood, such as how much compute should be spent rewriting training data and whether a model trained on rewrites can outperform the rewriter. We study these two questions by proposing a rewriting scaling law that predicts loss from model size, training tokens, the fraction of training tokens that are rewrites, and the number of epochs over the natural data, charging the compute spent generating rewrites. We fit this scaling law in two settings. In the compute-limited setting, natural data is plentiful and rewrites come from a third-party rewriter trained with more compute than our largest run. We find that the estimated optimal rewrite fraction increases with compute, from under $10\%$ at $3\times10^{18}$ FLOPs to $32\%$ at $10^{22}$ FLOPs. Our scaling forecast extrapolates $33\times$ in FLOPs to train a 22B-parameter model, whose final loss is within $1.1\%$ of our prediction. This model achieves a better BPB than its rewriter on several web domains at one-tenth the rewriter's training compute. In the data-limited setting, we assume we only have a fixed seed set of natural data and that our rewriter is a model of the same size as the target, pretrained on that seed set. Again, we find the optimal rewrite fraction increases with the compute budget. We also demonstrate that a model trained on rewrites of its own pretraining data, generated by a rewriter of the same size, beats the best run on natural data alone at every model size up to 3B. Our results show that synthetic rewriting can be studied through the scaling law lens: that optimal methodological choices are predictable and scaling does not necessarily break down beyond the rewriter's capabilities.


### [23] [Forecasting Associations, Not Interventions: A Preregistered Causal Audit of Covariate-Aware Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31869)
*Zong-Han Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Covariate-aware time series foundation models (TSFMs) accept a future path for a control variable and return a forecast, inviting what-if queries. The query is interventional; the models are trained on observational data. We determine whether their answers are the conditional slope $\partial E[Y|X]$ or the interventional effect $\partial E[Y|do(X)]$, at three levels. Theory: a Bayes-optimal observational predictor answers interventional queries associationally unless the adjustment set is available and used; for in-context least squares we derive the resulting confounding gap -- a model's effect estimate under an observational minus under a randomized context -- in closed form, a parameter-free reference line. Behaviour: on a preregistered grid with exact counterfactual ground truth, Chronos-2, TiRex-2, TabPFN-TS and TimesFM-2.5 all track this line ($R^2$ 0.90-1.00) and respond more confounded than regression itself (slopes 1.3-1.8), on linear and nonlinear structural models. Pairs of models with identical observational laws prove this is not an identifiability limit: when the effect is identifiable, least squares recovers 100% of the true effect difference, Chronos-2 75%, TiRex-2 34%. Representation: linear probes decode the data-generating regime (94-96%) and the true effect ($R^2$ 0.7-0.9) from embeddings that the forecasts ignore. On two physical systems with randomized actuators, pretrained models under-respond by 2-5x and ignore in-window confounding. Minutes of interventional post-training on 3,000 synthetic series cut the gap by up to 83% and transfer outside the training prior. Its forecasting cost is a replay-distribution effect: replaying the model's own pretraining prior cuts it from +47% to +10% MASE, and with low-rank adaptation yields a recipe meeting the preregistered criterion over three seeds (52-61% gap reduction at +1-3% cost) while improving the DoTime benchmark.


### [24] [Effectiveness of Curriculum Learning Depends on Reward Sparsity and Competing Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31884)
*John J. Vastola, Ann Huang, Satpreet H. Singh, Samuel J. Gershman, Kanaka Rajan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: While animals and people tend to learn a task more quickly and reliably when they first train on simpler versions of it, curriculum learning's effectiveness in artificial settings varies, and appears substantially greater in reinforcement learning than in supervised learning. In this work, we construct and analyze a minimal model of policy learning to better understand why. We consider an open-loop, episodic target interception task whose parameters---especially how close the agent must be to the target to receive an informative reward signal---can be chosen so that the agent either receives informative rewards frequently ('dense' rewards) or rarely ('sparse' rewards). We mathematically and numerically analyze the dynamics of REINFORCE-based policy learning in both cases. In the dense reward case, curricula have only marginal benefits; in the sparse case, curricula can be required for learning to occur at all. We find that this is especially true when the agent has the conflicting goals of minimizing effort and accumulating high reward, since this can produce distinct reward landscape optima which compete with one another. Our characterization of useful curricula in this setting indicates that they address two issues: they (i) make rewards less sparse to speed up learning, and (ii) shape the reward landscape to steer agents away from bad local optima.


### [25] [Scaling Transformer Energy-based Models For High Quality Image Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31889)
*Jungsoo Lee, Alexi Gladstone, Yilun Du*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Energy-Based Models (EBMs) are a flexible class of generative models that decouple probabilistic modeling from the generation process. This decoupling directly enables compositional generation and inference-time scaling, where sample quality can be refined simply by increasing sampling steps. However, severe challenges in high-dimensional sampling and training instabilities with deep architectures have restricted EBMs to low-dimensional regimes. To resolve these bottlenecks, we introduce the Transformer Energy-Based Model (TEM), a scalable transformer energy function operating entirely within a latent space. On top of this backbone, we extract energies at multiple layers and sum them, composing complementary constraints from local texture to global semantics. Furthermore, two components make this high-quality at scale: Positive Representation Alignment (PRA), which supervises TEM's intermediate representations with a vision foundation model, and advanced sampling strategies that improve sample quality at inference. TEM-XL achieves an FID-50K of 3.82 on class-conditional ImageNet 256$\times$256 without classifier-free guidance, showing that a contrastive EBM can generate high-fidelity images. On ImageNet 512$\times$512, TEM achieves an FID-50K of 8.24, demonstrating that the approach scales to higher resolution. We further validate the core promises of EBMs: compositional generation, zero-shot inpainting, and out-of-distribution detection.


### [26] [Learning Structured Sparsity for Large Models: Bridging Model Compressibility and Efficient Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31897)
*Tianxiang Xia, Kai Lion, Niao He, Michael Muehlebach, Melanie Zeilinger, Hao Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scaling laws continue to reward increases in model scale, while the resulting computational and memory demands drive greater reliance on cloud infrastructure for inference. Yet unreliable network connectivity, communication delays, and privacy requirements can make such reliance impractical, motivating local execution on edge devices. Local deployment requires models to fit within limited memory and execute efficiently with the available computational resources. Recent work has shown that learning sparse-plus-low-rank representations enables elastic compression of a single checkpoint to multiple memory budgets. However, fewer parameters do not necessarily translate into faster inference. Indexing overhead and irregular memory access in unstructured sparse components can offset computational savings. To address this critical deployment barrier, we introduce S-SALAAD, a unified framework that jointly learns the model weights and their low-rank and structured sparse components during training. The framework supports a family of hardware-compatible structured sparsity patterns. It couples this structure learning with budget-aware compression and dedicated CPU and GPU kernels that exploit the learned structure for acceleration, enabling efficient inference from a single checkpoint across memory budgets. Experiments on Llama2 and Qwen3 models, covering both pretraining and distillation, show that S-SALAAD advances the empirical Pareto frontier in perplexity, memory, and inference latency across multiple hardware platforms relative to the evaluated baselines. These results show that structured learning can translate elastic compressibility into reduced memory requirements and lower inference latency, enabling practical deployment of large models on edge devices.


### [27] [Topological Equivalence of Spectral and Density Cluster Hierarchies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31909)
*Sajjad Hashemian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We compare two canonical ways to recover hierarchical clustering from an underlying density $f$: the \emph{density cluster tree} given by connected components of superlevel sets $\{f\ge\lambda\}$, and a \emph{spectral hierarchy} obtained by applying single linkage to diffusion distances induced by a Laplacian operator. We give conditions under which, at an appropriate diffusion time, the diffusion hierarchy is \emph{topologically equivalent} to the density cluster tree up to a monotone rescaling of heights. It further shows that a $k$-NN graph implementation inherits this equivalence from uniform approximation of diffusion distances, yielding a finite-sample reduction from spectral graph constructions to density-level-set topology.


### [28] [Decode Late, Multiply Once: Rethinking the Dot Product for Quantized Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31913)
*Jonathan Tapson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large neural networks are increasingly deployed with 1-4 bit weights and 16- or 32-bit activations, yet their dot products still use one multiplier per element. We show that a length-n dot product with k-bit weight codes can be computed with n+2^k additions and at most 2^k decodes and 2^k multiplications. Each weight code is used directly as an address offset that selects one of 2^k accumulators; each activation is added into the selected accumulator; and the accumulated sums are decoded and multiplied only once, at the end, in a fused decode-and-multiply step. The decoded weight vector is never formed. The refactoring is exact for integer arithmetic, works with integer or floating-point operands and with arbitrary, including nonlinear, decoders, and extends to group-wise scales and to matrix and tensor products. The multiplier count falls by n/2^k, while total arithmetic energy improves by a smaller, bounded factor that we quantify with a cost model. We describe a scatter-accumulate processing element (SA-PE) that replaces the MAC unit in a weight-stationary systolic array, with a load/stream/reduce/drain schedule in which a small, time-shareable multiplier runs only 2^k times per tile. Because decoded weights are not stored, local weight memory shrinks by up to w_d/k for w_d-bit decoded weights. An RTL implementation synthesized for FPGA and compared with a matched MAC array shows that an unshared SA-PE is larger than a MAC PE, and that the saving appears when the reduction multiplier is shared across PEs. Software models confirm exact integer equivalence and floating-point error no worse than the conventional pipeline. We do not claim the underlying identity is new, and we discuss related work on weight sharing and lookup-based kernels.


### [29] [Every Weight Is a Path of Shared Discrete Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31931)
*Ammar Ali, Stamatis Lefkimmiatis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce TWIG (Tree-structured Weights with Inherited Gates), a representation in which every weight of a neural network is the end of a path of small discrete decisions: a sign, a shift by a power of two, a zero gate, an index into a shared table. Weights that belong together share the upper part of their paths, and each decision is inherited by every weight below it, so it is stored once and applied once. This simple change has three consequences. Any trained network can be written as a TWIG, and when the decisions are powers of two its weight layers become circuits made almost entirely of wires and adders, suited to chips whose weights are fixed at fabrication. Pruning, quantization, low-rank factorization, codebooks and weight sharing become edits of one object, so a single optimizer can choose among all of them for a whole network. And choosing the decisions is the ground-state problem of a higher-order Boltzmann machine whose interactions follow the paths of the tree, the problem class that annealers and quantum optimizers are built to solve. Today these questions are handled apart: each compression method brings its own format and solver, a circuit with fixed weights needs a dedicated constant multiplier for every weight, and discrete solvers reach only narrow subproblems such as rounding. Weight formats have long been moving toward shared decisions, through group scales, shared exponents, microexponents and sparse weights; TWIG takes this trend to its end, and every common format is a shallow TWIG at no extra cost. On five weight matrices of a large language model, a flat tree whose signed power-of-two terms are chosen per weight for circuit cost, with no group scales, needs 9--15\% fewer additions than GPTQ at equal layer error at 3- and 4-bit accuracy on typical matrices, and 20--55\% fewer on matrices with strong outliers.


### [30] [Beyond Local Accuracy: Benchmarking the Rollout Reliability of PDE Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31935)
*Jaewan Park, Seid Koric, Giri Krishnan, Sudhakar Pamidighantam, Surresh Marru*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: PDE foundation models enable data-efficient adaptation but are deployed autoregressively, so short-horizon accuracy need not imply reliable simulation. We ask whether the short-horizon advantage of a foundation-model recipe persists when its predictions are repeatedly fed back as inputs for a long horizon. We define error amplification as G=E(H_f)/E(H_s), which measures full-horizon error relative to short-horizon error. We evaluate seven models on seven two-dimensional time-dependent PDE datasets: scratch-trained FNO and DeepONet; fine-tuned DPOT, GPhyT, Poseidon, and Walrus; and frozen in-context VICON. We report VRMSE, relative L_2, and relative MAE. Six datasets provide paired short- and full-horizon scores, while CE-RM contributes short-rollout sweeps only. At least one fine-tuned foundation model outperforms both scratch baselines in 26 of 30 short-rollout dataset--budget cells under every metric. Nevertheless, full-horizon VRMSE exceeds short-horizon VRMSE in 140 of 144 paired trained configurations, with a median error amplification of $2.04$. The count below VRMSE one falls from 104 to 53, and 12 full rollouts exceed ten although none does at the short horizon. Walrus achieves the lowest full-horizon VRMSE in 16 of 24 cells, yet its median error amplification is $2.70$. Relative L_2 and relative MAE show the same qualitative pattern. These results show that favorable short-horizon rankings can coexist with substantial error amplification. PDE foundation-model benchmarks should therefore report horizon-resolved absolute errors, amplification statistics, and failure incidence rather than a single local forecast score.


### [31] [Agreement rather than normalisation keeps depth reusable in looped language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31943)
*Ivan Viakhirev, Kirill Borodin, Maxim Abramov, Grach Mkrtchian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looped language models apply one block of layers repeatedly, and the design pays off only if depth is reusable: loop steps beyond the trained budget must remain usable at inference. We ask whether that rests on normalising the state between loop steps, as the released Ouro models do, or on agreement between the loop a model is trained with and the loop it is run with. On released weights this loop-boundary normalisation can be removed without adding parameters and with the readout path bit-identical, and the operation is an identity at one loop step, which serves as an implementation check. Removing it takes Ouro-1.4B and Ouro-2.6B to zero accuracy by the trained budget on a balanced binary task (chance 0.500). State-norm growth, the failure that prior accounts predict, is not the cause: the norm stays within a factor of 2.021 while the head commits to a single wrong token. Yet in 19M-parameter models trained from scratch on two synthetic tasks, 16 seeds per configuration, training without the normalisation is never worse than training with it and extrapolates better: the two are closest at the trained depth and separate on both sides of it (Cliff's delta up to 0.867 beyond the budget). Both results follow if agreement, and not the normalisation itself, is what keeps depth reusable. The damage sits in the readout together with the last layer of the block: in the models trained from scratch, refitting the head over the frozen modified loop recovers a third of the lost accuracy and plateaus, while refitting the last block layer with it recovers all of it. Finally, comparing looped with stacked depth at an equal inference loop count is confounded by unequal overshoot of the trained budget; at matched overshoot the advantage of looping vanishes on one task and shrinks on the other.


### [32] [Towards Trustworthy AI-Enabled Emergency Medical Dispatch: Uncertainty-Aware Ambulance Travel Time Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31944)
*Muhammad Adil Abid, Johan Holmgren, Fabian Lorig, Jesper Petersson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Emergency medical dispatch increasingly relies on AI-enabled decision support systems when making ambulance dispatch decisions. In this process, accurate ambulance travel time estimates are essential for timely resource allocation and prehospital care. However, existing ambulance travel time estimation approaches typically produce only single point predictions. Consequently, they do not indicate how reliable an individual estimate is or how much uncertainty it carries. This limitation is particularly problematic in safety-critical emergency medical service dispatch operations, where trips with similar expected travel times may exhibit substantially different levels of uncertainty. Furthermore, real-world ambulance trip logs may contain domain-specific artefacts, such as co-located healthcare facilities and physically implausible travel speeds. These artefacts can degrade point prediction accuracy and affect the prediction task on which subsequent uncertainty estimation depends. To address these challenges, we propose an uncertainty-aware methodology for ambulance travel time estimation and evaluate it through a retrospective real-world scenario study using two years of ambulance trip data from the Skåne region in Sweden. The proposed methodology moves beyond average-case point predictions by generating context-adaptive prediction intervals with stable empirical coverage across the evaluated operationally important contexts. The resulting prediction intervals provide explicit information about predictive uncertainty that can support more transparent and risk-sensitive ambulance dispatch decision-making.


### [33] [Sharp Bounds on the Winner's Bias under Independent and Nonnegatively Correlated Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31948)
*Ivan Viakhirev, Kirill Borodin, Grach Mkrtchian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reporting the best of $M$ noisy, unbiased scores overstates the winner's value by up to $c\sqrt{V}$, where $V$ is the total noise variance and the sharp constant $c$ depends on how the noise is dependent. It was known only at the two extremes, arbitrary dependence ($\sqrt{(M-1)/M}$) and i.i.d. noise. We determine it for two classes in between: for independent, not identically distributed noise it is $\sqrt{(M-1)/(2M)}$, and for pairwise nonnegatively correlated noise it is $(M-1)/M$; for uncorrelated noise this answers the maximum version of an open question about the expected range. Explicit finite constructions attain both. As a corollary, the bias of the cross-validation estimator is bounded sharply within each class. All theorems are formally verified in Lean 4. Across 448 language-model and classifier selection pipelines, designs with per-candidate items realize a median 0.75 of the independent-class ceiling. At a fixed item budget, scoring all candidates on shared items is markedly less optimistic than giving each candidate its own items.


### [34] [A Mathematical Theory of the Jacobian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31955)
*Mitchell Ostrow, Daoyuan Qian, Toni Liu, Ila Fiete*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work from Anthropic introduced a new interpretability tool, called the Jacobian lens (J-lens, Gurnee et al., 2026). This method identified linear directions in the hidden states of frontier language models that had properties reminiscent of working memory-like representations. However, it is unclear what mechanistic properties of such features make them detectable to the J-lens. To this end, we introduce a mathematical theory of the J-lens, and show that it preferentially identifies coherent features -- directions that act like leading, spatially coherent Lyapunov modes. Small perturbations along these directions amplify across layers, remain roughly constant across token positions, and are consistent across different contexts. While we are agnostic to whether these properties are necessarily associated with a ``Global Workspace'', the analytically derived preferences of the J-lens nevertheless explain prior empirical applied to both model classes. We validate our analysis on a Bayes filter and a toy transformer, each optimally predicting next tokens emitted from a Hidden Markov Model (HMM). We show how modulating task parameters that change the coherence of features in the Bayes filter correspondingly and predictively modulates the response of the J-lens . We also introduce of a family of J-lenses built to emphasize different temporal scales of information processing in LLMs, which we call $\omega$-J-lenses.


### [35] [Beyond Heuristics and Commercial Navigation: An AI-Augmented Decision-Support for Ambulance Travel Time Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31968)
*Muhammad Adil Abid, Johan Holmgren, Fabian Lorig, Jesper Petersson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate ambulance travel time estimation is critical for emergency medical dispatch, yet existing decision-support practices often rely on static lookup tables or simplified assumptions regarding how ambulance travel time differs from ordinary passenger-car travel. For instance, they assume ambulances simply travel a fixed percentage (e.g., 5%) faster than ordinary passenger cars. However, such assumptions are rarely validated using real ambulance operational data and may fail to capture the effects of trip priority, temporal context, and origin-destination. We address these limitations using historical ambulance trips from the Skåne region in Sweden. Our analysis reveals that ambulance travel times vary substantially by hour, day, and trip priority, and that commercial navigation systems fail to capture emergency-specific dynamics. We contribute a machine learning system to more accurately estimate ambulance travel time. To make the system interpretable, we explored SHapley Additive exPlanations and permutation importance techniques to ensure that our system captures operationally meaningful patterns.


### [36] [Compatible Likelihoods for Flow Matching on Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31981)
*Lucas Ng, Georgios Batzolis, Mark Girolami*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On the simplest non-Euclidean manifold, the 1D flat torus, we exhibit a distribution where a recently proposed extension of Riemannian flow matching is globally optimized by a velocity field that pushes samples away from the data distribution. We trace this failure mode to a property we call compatibility; a likelihood-based flow matching objective is compatible if its population minimizer recovers the marginal velocity field induced by the data and coupling. We give a general condition for compatibility, apply it to existing methods, and bound the worst-case error in terms of a residual component vanishing under compatibility. Next, we note that existing compatible objectives put density on velocities that are infeasible on compact manifolds. We propose an objective built from an exponential family over endpoints, with the conditional velocity as sufficient statistic, which is both compatible and supported only on feasible velocities. This manifests as a reweighting of the flow matching loss which, on constant-curvature manifolds, treats errors in flow speed and direction differently. Empirically, our method recovers the correct velocity field on the test case, outperforms prior methods on high-dimensional product tori, and obtains substantial NLL improvements over prior work on two of four standard geospatial benchmarks on the sphere at moderate runtime overhead. On a large dataset of high-dimensional human poses, our method outperforms prior work according to sample-based metrics.


### [37] [REDAR: Reinforcement-Driven Adaptive Regularisation for Class Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31991)
*Akila Ayanthi, Darshana Priyasad, Tharindu Fernando, Sridha Sridharan, Clinton Fookes, Peyman Moghadam*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Regularisation-based class-incremental learning (CIL) methods control the stability-plasticity trade-off through fixed coefficients tuned offline, independently of the continual learning objective and blind to phase-specific variations in data volume, task difficulty, and class similarity. We argue this is a fundamental mismatch and propose REDAR: Reinforcement-Driven Adaptive Regularisation for Class Incremental Learning, which treats regularisation strength selection as a sequential decision problem. At each incremental phase, a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) agent observes a compact state of current performance metrics and incoming data statistics, and outputs the distillation coefficients for that phase. To respect CIL's constraint on historical data access, the policy is trained entirely on pseudo-CIL tasks constructed from the initial phase, then deployed on the incremental sequence without further adaptation. Experiments on CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet demonstrate that REDAR reduces forgetting and improves forward transfer over fixed and heuristic baselines, and that policies trained on short pseudo-task horizons transfer effectively to longer incremental runs.


### [38] [GS-MoE: Training-Free MoE Compression via Groupwise Subspace Sharing and Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32009)
*Carson Cary, Yukai Jin, Yihao Huang, Zhankai Ye, Fangyi Li, Ting Hua, Xin Liu, Shangqian Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models reduce computation through sparse activation, but storing their expert parameters remains a major memory bottleneck. We introduce GS-MoE, a training-free compression framework that addresses redundancy across and within experts through two compression axes: shared-subspace rank and expert intermediate width. Groupwise low-rank representations capture shared structure across experts while retaining expert-specific projections, and Nyström-based neuron compression reduces intermediate width within each expert. For fixed shared subspaces, we derive per-expert reconstruction-error reductions from the reconstruction objective and use them to guide expert grouping. Budgets are allocated hierarchically using a gradient-free layer-importance score to guide the allocation of rank and width budgets across layers, which eigenvalues and leverage then distribute as non-uniform ranks across groups and intermediate widths across experts. Experiments demonstrate the complementary benefits of compression along both axes, particularly at high compression rates on fine-grained MoE models. On DeepSeek-MoE-16B-Base, at a 60% reduction in total model parameters, GS-MoE retains approximately 90% of the uncompressed model's average reasoning performance, compared with 73% for D$^2$-MoE, while achieving 20% lower out-of-domain perplexity. We provide a code implementation at: this https URL


### [39] [Rethinking Representation Geometry in Supervised Contrastive Learning under Long-Tailed Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32039)
*Joonhyeok Jeong, Jy-yong Sohn, Kibok Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Supervised contrastive learning and its balanced variants have demonstrated their effectiveness for representation learning under long-tailed distributions. However, although prior analyses suggest that their optimal representations may exhibit a regular simplex geometry, whether such a geometry is indeed exhibited remains insufficiently understood. In this work, we present a theoretical framework for these objectives that characterizes whether a regular simplex is formed at the optimum. By applying this to representative objectives, we show that these objectives fail to form a regular simplex at the optimum because they remain dependent on the number of samples per class. Motivated by this analysis, we introduce structural balancing that can be applied to contrastive objectives, and propose a fully balanced contrastive objective that provably forms a regular simplex geometry at its optimum. Both theoretical and empirical results demonstrate that the proposed method yields a more balanced representation space and improves performance on long-tailed recognition and transfer learning tasks.


### [40] [Controlled Evaluation of EEG Foundation Models: Random Initialisation, Band-Power and Validation-Selected Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32067)
*Mingyu Jeon, Suwan Cho, Jae Young Suh*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: EEG foundation models report downstream gains, yet their protocols seldom include three controls. These are the same encoder untrained, spectral features fitted on the same data, and a layer picked without looking at test scores. We therefore evaluate five publicly released EEG foundation models and an in-house JEPA-style model under one protocol with all three controls and several seeds, on five benchmarks and four added task families. The controls change the reading in three ways. First, picking the probed layer by its test score raises the layer-wise peak by an average of +0.034 and reverses the peak-versus-output gap in 9 of 36 cells. Second, with the layer picked on validation, random initialisation matches or beats pretraining for half of the models on sleep and emotion, and no model beats a five-band log-power baseline on emotion or motor imagery. Third, after full fine-tuning only 5 of 30 pairs gain beyond twice their seed spread, and the one clear loss of -0.290 AUROC persists under matched learning rates. From these results we draw four reporting practices, and we release every cell for re-analysis.


### [41] [DBCM: Dual-Branch Calibration Module for Test-Time Adaptation in Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32071)
*Jaebyeong Jeon, Kunwoo Kang, Sang Yup Lee, Kibok Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Non-stationarity induces test-time distribution shifts in time series forecasting (TSF), resulting in forecast residuals between predictions and the corresponding ground truth. Recent works on test-time adaptation for TSF address this by introducing lightweight calibration modules around frozen forecasters, updated using observations that become incrementally available at test time. Existing approaches typically employ channel-wise adapters, implicitly assuming that forecast residuals are inherently channel-specific. However, we observe that forecast residuals often exhibit substantial shared structure across channels, suggesting that fully independent channel-wise parameterization may be redundant. Motivated by this observation, we propose the Dual-Branch Calibration Module (DBCM), which shares adaptation capacity across channels through a shared adapter while retaining limited channel-specific flexibility through a low-rank channel-wise adapter, further enhanced by channel mixing and nonlinear gated modulation. Extensive experiments show that DBCM consistently outperforms prior methods across diverse datasets, horizons, and backbones.


### [42] [PRISM: Promoting Sampling Diversity of Large Language Models with Implicit Stochastic Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32101)
*Qixin Xu, Zihan Zhu, Xiaojiang Liu, Bohan Lyu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit mode collapse, where their output distribution concentrates on familiar semantic modes. When diversity disappears, repeated sampling becomes semantic repetition rather than discovery, making rare but stronger solutions unlikely to surface and leaving self-evolution and continual learning difficult to sustain. Most remedies intervene only at inference time, shaping sam- ples without making diversity a model capability. We propose PRISM, a training-time framework that internalizes diversity within the model. PRISM combines (i) submodular curation that pairs each prompt with diverse-yet-correct multi-teacher solutions, and (ii) stochastic embedding modulation, which turns special-token perturbations into implicit signals for sampling distinct outputs. A theoretical analysis establishes the stability of this mechanism. On WritingPrompts and BookMIA, PRISM moves the quality-diversity frontier outward, achieving the best generation quality in all model-benchmark settings while maintaining the strongest or near-strongest semantic diversity across two open-weight model families. It also improves sampling efficiency on math and code benchmarks. Beyond inference, PRISM yields a stronger foundation for reinforcement learning, sustaining diverse rollouts and mitigating the diversity collapse commonly observed during RL training.


### [43] [ReMedI: Inference-Time Refinement and Self-Distillation for Controllable Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32103)
*Jia Yi Zhang, Shivanshu Shekhar, Allison McLevige, Tong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In drug development, lead optimization must improve competing properties, such as increasing permeability and reducing toxicity, while limiting scaffold changes that could compromise the lead's potency. RL-based post-training, such as C-MORAL, addresses this problem by fine-tuning molecular language models to directly optimize molecular leads for objectives of interest, which relies on expensive task-specific datasets and faces challenges in achieving multi-objective optimization (MOO). We argue a pre-trained model may already contain useful molecular edits that zero-shot generation cannot efficiently reach. Motivated by the limited search capabilities of zero-shot generation, we develop an efficient Metropolis-Hastings search strategy to better explore the proposal distribution. We propose ReMedI, which uses the molecular language model as a base proposer for inference-time alignment. Given a lead and a set of multi-objective tasks, the model proposes edits conditioned on the current molecule, while Metropolis--Hastings refinement guides sequential edits under an objective for property improvement, preservation, and lead similarity. On both C-MuMOInstruct and SMDD benchmarks, refinement improves frozen base models across three backbones, reaching significantly higher multi-objective optimization success rates compared to baselines. These search traces can then distilled into the base model which improves zero-shot generation and subsequent search using approximately 7.8 times less training molecular leads than that of C-MORAL. ReMedI achieves approximately 2.8 times the strict-success coverage of RL baseline C-MORAL, including on out-of-distribution tasks, with matched lead-relative similarity.


### [44] [Putting Transformer Theories to the Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32104)
*Awni Altabaa, Tobias Schnabel, Kiran Tomlinson*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Several formal theories have been developed to characterize the capabilities and limitations of the transformer architecture, each employing different abstractions that capture different features of the architecture. Due to the differences in their underlying assumptions and conceptual frameworks, these theories can disagree in their predictions about what tasks transformers find easy or hard. We map out seven theories of transformer capability (Star-free, $\mathsf{AC}^0$, $\mathsf{TC}^0$, C-RASP, MPC, BAPO, and Sensitivity) and translate their results into a common matrix of predictions across a suite of tasks covering different computational structures. We provide new theoretical results to complete this matrix. We then evaluate theoretical predictions through controlled experiments measuring in-distribution sample complexity, length generalization, and pretrained language model performance. We find that no single theory explains the observed pattern of successes and failures. When a theory does not capture the transformer behaviors relevant to a task, its predictions break down; for instance, we show that the $k$-hop induction task is predicted hard by C-RASP because it does not model content-dependent attention, but we train small transformers on this task that length-generalize. Similarly, theories assuming hard attention like $\mathsf{AC}^0$ and BAPO predict binary majority to be hard, for which we also find success in practice. Together, our results point to several specific features that a theory of transformers must capture: soft rather than hard attention, dynamic content-dependent retrieval, bounded computation at each position, an account of learnability and inductive biases, and a combination of upper and lower bounds.


### [45] [REACT: Representational Early Analysis and Control of Chain-of-Thought Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32113)
*Hanwen Zhang, Enyi Jiang, Ruike Zhu, Qinan Yu, Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) generation offers an opportunity to intervene before a reasoning model completes its answer. But does the point at which risk is easiest to detect also offer the most effective control? We study this question with REACT, a conditional activation intervention that learns a safety direction from paired safe and unsafe trajectories and applies it once to an early CoT hidden state. The model weights remain frozen, and all subsequent tokens are generated without further edits. To study timing, we train directions separately at each of the first five CoT positions and evaluate ungated edits with full post-edit regeneration. Across three distilled reasoning models and three generation seeds, the strongest gains concentrate in a two- to three-token early window; on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, the effect falls to approximately zero at the fourth and fifth tokens, even though later states can be more predictive of risk. This separates risk readability from effective control: intervention timing must be evaluated through its effect on subsequent generation. On the same 8B model, REACT raises the rate of fully safe CoT-answer outputs on StrongREJECT-273 from 29.06% to 82.05%, while keeping benign refusal and usefulness near baseline. An anonymized implementation is available at this https URL .


### [46] [Beyond Data Schedule: Fast LLM Pretraining on Data Mixtures by Loss-Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32141)
*Jiazheng Li, Huaqing Zhang, Jing Xu, Tianle Cai, Jingzhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models often acquire specialized capabilities through successive pretraining stages, but these gains can come at the cost of forgetting earlier knowledge. In this work, we investigate whether such trade-offs can be improved by changing how strongly each domain contributes to learning while keeping its data exposure fixed. We find that, in controlled knowledge--mathematics experiments, loss reweighting produces a single checkpoint that surpasses the best knowledge and reasoning scores attained along either stage trajectory. This reveals an independent control that data-mixture ratios alone do not capture. Building on this finding, we propose LARE (Loss-Adaptive Reweighting under fixed Exposure), which automatically adjusts domain weights from running loss statistics. Motivated by a theoretical analysis of gradient noise, its normalized inverse-square-root rule balances relative loss scales without changing the sampled mixture or requiring additional runs to search for weights. Across knowledge, mathematics, and code, \Method{} improves all five evaluated benchmarks for a 1.5B model trained on 240B tokens, averaging +7.84 pp over a matched direct mixture, including gains of +17.06 pp on GSM8K and +9.00 pp on MATH. These findings demonstrate that adjusting domain loss scales can improve joint capabilities without additional domain exposure.


### [47] [Rethinking LoRA Initialization for Robust Asymmetric Learning Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32142)
*Disen Liao, Yaoliang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is often highly sensitive to learning-rate (LR) choices. Recent empirical studies suggest that, once LRs are carefully tuned, vanilla LoRA can remain competitive with many proposed LoRA variants. This shifts the central practical question from designing ever more variants to reducing the cost and brittleness of LR selection. LoRA+ addresses this issue with asymmetric LRs, using a larger LR for the up-projection matrix $B$ than for the down-projection matrix $A$. However, the ratio $\eta_B / \eta_A$ itself becomes an additional hyperparameter: large ratios can improve adaptation, but under the standard LoRA initialization they can also cause unstable training and ratio-dependent performance collapse. We identify the source of this brittleness through a signal--noise decomposition of LoRA dynamics. Under standard \initA, the update contains an initialization-induced noise term whose magnitude can be affected by the LR for $B$, $\eta_B$, the initialization scale $\beta$, and the adapter rank $r$. In practical LLM fine-tuning, this non-vanishing noise can limit the performance gains expected from LoRA+ style asymmetric LRs. To suppress this effect, we propose \initAA, a simple initialization that scales the variance of $A$ as $\sigma_A^2 \propto n^{-2}$ while keeping $B_0=0$. Under matched rank and training time for both initializations, this reduces the initialization-noise energy by an additional factor of $1/n$, stabilizing asymmetric LoRA training while preserving zero initial adapter output. Empirically, \initAA substantially widens the stable LR region across initialization scales, adapter ranks, and LoRA update multipliers. Across tasks and models, \initAA reduces best-ratio drift, prevents the high-ratio collapse observed with standard \initA, and makes the width-based guideline $\eta_B \approx n \eta_A$ a useful starting point for finite pretrained models.


### [48] [Sparsity-driven emergence of functional modularity in multitask neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32153)
*Bowen Shi, Qi Ouyang, Long Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural networks can learn multiple tasks, but how learning organizes shared computation into specialized and reusable components remains poorly understood. In this work, we show that sparsity inducing regularization promotes functional modularity in multitask networks without predefined module boundaries. Using functional profiling in multilayer perceptrons and a lightweight transformer, we identified compact, task-selective subnetworks, termed functional tokens. Across rule-based task families, selectively retaining these tokens preserves their associated functions while reducing performance on other tasks. Such specialization develops preferentially in deeper layers during training, while earlier layers retain shared computation. Regularization strength and form shape module compactness and specialization, while task weighting alters functional allocation. Moreover, extracted functional tokens can be recombined through alternative pathways to reconstruct a learned composite computation without retraining, preserving their connectivity and learned weights. Our results suggest that sparsity-constrained optimization shapes not only parameter use but also the organization and reuse of learned computation.


### [49] [Curvature-Aware Clipping: Mitigating Bias in DPSGD via Approximate Second-Order Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32175)
*Xincheng Xu, Thilina Ranbaduge, Thierry Rakotoarivelo, David Smith*

Main category: cs.LG
