Abstract: Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DPSGD) has become the de facto standard for training deep neural networks with rigorous privacy guarantees. However, the utility of DPSGD is constrained by gradient clipping bias and DP noise. While existing work has tried to address these issues, the geometric misalignment caused by clipping bias remains a critical challenge. To bridge this gap, we propose a novel framework, Curvature-Aware Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (CA-DPSGD), which leverages approximate second-order information to construct an optimal, curvature-aware clipping space. CA-DPSGD integrates a rectified estimator for the curvature matrix and is coupled with an anisotropic noise mechanism to ensure privacy guarantees. Extensive empirical experiments demonstrate that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art clipping methods.


### [50] [Searching with Sparse Histories: Cross-Task Architecture Search from Incomplete Search Logs and Zero-Cost Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32179)
*Yuefeng Xu, Jun Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) is expensive, but cross-task transfer can reduce costs by reusing knowledge from previous tasks. However, existing methods struggle with sparse search histories and task-specific transfer failures. We propose a method, a risk-robust strategy that combines sparse source logs with zero-cost proxies through champion-based initialization and joint pairwise-ranker pretraining. On TransNAS-Bench-101, the proposal mitigates severe worst-case failures, reducing worst-10% ranks from 290 to 43 for Micro autoencoding and from 465 to 45 for Macro room layout. Across 13 cell-space targets, it outperforms a proxy-only ranker on 10 targets in both mean and worst-case performance, while maintaining competitive average performance.


### [51] [CFBrainAug: Modularity-Induced Counterfactual Brain Network Augmentation for Interpretable fMRI-based Autism Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32184)
*Yifan Qiao, Sheng Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine learning on resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI) brain networks is promising for diagnosing Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD), but existing methods are prone to overfitting on limited training data, and their post-hoc statistical attribution offers limited clinical interpretability. We propose CFBrainAug, a counterfactual brain network learning framework that unifies data augmentation and counterfactual explanation by learning label-altered brain networks. Unlike conventional label-preserving augmentation, CFBrainAug first retrieves a label-opposite yet topologically similar candidate and then refines it under a module-number constraint. Jointly training on factual and counterfactual networks enriches the training distribution around the decision boundary, enabling a lightweight feature-selection-based SVM to perform competitively with recent graph deep learning methods. Meanwhile, the counterfactuals provide intrinsic explanations by identifying the connectivity differences between each subject and its counterfactual. Experiments on the ABIDE I dataset show that CFBrainAug reaches an accuracy of 74.1% on the NYU site, suggesting that counterfactual augmentation can complement more complex models in small-sample brain network analysis.


### [52] [EVOLVE: Uncertainty-Aware Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Prediction with Adaptive Prototype Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32189)
*Jianxin Jin, Kai Zhao, Shiyan Hu, Yang Shu, Peng Chen, Meng Wang, Zhongwen Rao, Yijie Li, Jing Wang, Bin Yang, Chenjuan Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Unsupervised multivariate time series anomaly prediction (AP) aims to estimate future anomaly risks from current observations, providing early-warning signals for proactive intervention and maintenance in complex systems. Existing unsupervised AP methods follow a perturbation-based paradigm, where predefined or random perturbations are injected into historical windows to simulate abnormal future evolution. However, these methods share perturbations across different variables and contexts, and learn deterministic history-to-future evolution. In reality, anomaly evolution is variable- and context-dependent: the same perturbation may be amplified into abnormal future dynamics in one variable or context, while being absorbed as normal fluctuation in another. In addition, future evolution is not uniquely determined by a historical window, since similar histories may lead to multiple plausible trajectories, such as gradual degradation or abrupt changes. To address these problems, we propose EVOLVE, a unified framework that models unsupervised AP via variable- and context-conditioned perturbations and uncertainty-aware future reasoning. EVOLVE constructs context-specific anomaly perturbations through an adaptive anomaly prototype memory, which can dynamically update and retrieve relevant perturbations at the variable level. EVOLVE models uncertainty by sampling multiple latent historical states and decoding through complementary smooth and deviation-amplifying evolution hypotheses, and the disagreement among decoded future trajectories is used to estimate future anomaly risk. Extensive experiments on six real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that EVOLVE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art anomaly prediction baselines.


### [53] [Dual-Adaptive Z-Score PPO: Dynamic Clipping Using Action Confidence and Normalized Advantage Magnitude](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32194)
*Taha M. Mahmoud, Naima Kaabouch*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) remains a popular on-policy reinforcement learning algorithm owing to its stability and ease of implementation. Its clipping threshold, however, is fixed, so every training sample receives the same trust-region width regardless of how reliable the corresponding policy update is. This work examines adaptive-clipping variants of PPO in which the constant clipping parameter is replaced by sample-dependent thresholds computed from policy and return statistics. The central method, Dual-Adaptive Z-Score PPO (PPO-DAZ3), sets the clipping interval for each sample from two signals: action confidence, given by the probability of the selected action under the current policy, and update confidence, given by the normalized advantage magnitude. The rest of the standard PPO training pipeline is left unchanged. Evaluation on three classic control benchmarks (LunarLander-v3, CartPole-v1, and Acrobot-v1) indicates that adaptive clipping improves learning in some settings but not all. PPO-DAZ3 performs best on LunarLander-v3, the smooth advantage-based variant PPO-AZ3S leads on Acrobot-v1, and standard PPO stays competitive on CartPole-v1, suggesting that adaptive clipping is most beneficial on harder control tasks.


### [54] [Principia: Test-Time Hypotheses to Principle Construction for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32195)
*Dongki Kim, Sangwoo Park, Jinheon Baek, Soyeong Jeong, Sung Ju Hwang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scientific discovery requires identifying the scientific principles underlying observed phenomena to guide further investigation. As these empirical principles are often unknown in complex discovery processes, they are constructed via a hypotheses-to-principle pipeline to obtain recurring causalities from progressively accumulated observations during test-time discovery. However, constructing this knowledge requires substantial domain expertise, time, and effort, making this translation stage a bottleneck for scientific discovery. To address this limitation, we propose Principia, an LLM-based framework that constructs and continually refines scientific principles at test time. Drawing on how human researchers develop principles, Principia forms comparative causal hypotheses from related inputs and their observed outcomes, then synthesizes recurring relationships across these hypotheses into inductive principles. By applying the constructed hypotheses and inductive principles to the task, it obtains new observations, revises the hypotheses used based on these new observations, and reconstructs principles from the updated hypotheses, allowing scientific knowledge to continuously evolve throughout test-time discovery. Across benchmarks spanning molecular optimization, protein engineering, gene perturbation, and molecular property prediction, Principia outperforms baselines relying on internal knowledge, external sources, or experience summaries, while generating principles that are plausible and consistent with the scientific literature, demonstrating its effectiveness in constructing and refining task-specific hypotheses and principles at test time for scientific discovery. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [55] [Head Dimension Matters in Language Models for Long-Context Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32213)
*Seiya Tokui, Shoichiro Yamaguchi, Sosuke Kobayashi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multi-head attention retrieves and combines context representations through query--key interactions and value aggregation, with head dimension constraining the per-head representation space. In long-context processing, it remains unclear whether allocating a fixed parameter budget to fewer, wider heads improves information retrieval. In this paper, we investigate how head dimension affects the performance of long-context retrieval tasks. Our empirical studies reveal that pretraining loss and short-context non-retrieval task performance are not affected much by head dimension, while long-context retrieval performance improves with wider heads at fixed model sizes. We find that models with wider heads have a higher proportion of retrieval heads, associating more parameters with retrieval. Our ablation studies also show that the retrieval performance improves with wider heads even when the projection weights in attention modules are frozen during long-context continual pretraining, suggesting that wider heads not only benefit updates in attention modules but lead to more effective adaptation of components such as embeddings and MLPs.


### [56] [MS-TTRL: Breaking the Frequency Conflict in Multiple-Solution Test-Time Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32234)
*Zhixia Zhang, Binqian Xu, Huaiyang Wang, Yikun Ban, Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scientific, engineering, and mathematical reasoning tasks can admit multiple valid solutions, while exhaustive annotation of the full solution set is often prohibitively costly. Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) applies reinforcement learning to unlabeled test instances, offering a label-free approach to multiple-solution reasoning. In this setting, generation frequency serves two opposing objectives: correctness estimation favors frequent outcomes, whereas outcome-level diversity preservation favors less frequent outcomes. We term this tension the \textbf{Frequency Conflict}. To resolve this conflict, we propose MS-TTRL, which obtains frequency-independent correctness estimates through Outcome-Level Self-Verification while reserving generation frequency solely for diversity preservation. Three-State Outcome Cache and the Candidate State support verification reuse across repeated observations and confidence-aware handling of ambiguous outcomes, while Confidence-Gated Inverse Probability Scaling selectively strengthens the learning signals of rare, reliably verified outcomes. Experiments across two benchmarks, four multiple-solution tasks, and three backbone models, each evaluated with five random seeds, show that MS-TTRL improves Recovery Rate by 19.13 percentage points on average over the best-performing label-free baseline and approaches the performance of the supervised IPS-GRPO.


### [57] [Correct but Unselectable: The Hidden Interface Tax in Multi-Candidate Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32235)
*Qianli Ma, Zhiqing Tang, Fanshuai Meng, Zhi Yao, Hanshuai Cui, Weijia Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Small language models deployed under cost, latency, or privacy constraints cannot simply switch to larger models when reasoning fails. Multi-candidate generate-then-rerank offers a natural test-time scaling route. However, more candidates do not always yield higher accuracy. We identify a previously overlooked bottleneck between generation and selection: a correct answer may appear in the candidate pool yet fail to be selected because it is incomplete, unparseable, or misaligned with the answer mode the fixed selector expects. We term this loss the hidden interface tax and formalize a coverage-conversion decomposition that distinguishes raw oracle coverage from selectable coverage, the subset of problems where a correct candidate can actually be consumed by the selector. We propose Compatibility-Aware Candidate Construction (CACC), an interface layer between the candidate source and the fixed selector that repairs fragments, aligns answer modes, and removes selector-confusing noise without retraining either component. On numeric reasoning (GSM8K, competition_math), CACC raises both oracle coverage and final accuracy. On GPQA Diamond, CACC alone improves final accuracy from 5.6% to 19.2% (+13.6 pp); combined with proposer-side strengthening it reaches 25.8% (+20.2 pp). On MMLU-Pro, CACC exposes a coverage-conversion gap: answer-mode-compatible construction raises oracle coverage, but final accuracy decreases by 0.85 pp under the original selector; adding proposer-side strengthening improves final accuracy.


### [58] [BiLoCo: Binary Low-Rank Corrections for Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32243)
*David Jin, Beshr IslamBouli, Tarushii Goel, Han Guo, Yoon Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) to FP4 has emerged as a key technique for reducing large language model inference cost, especially on Blackwell GPUs, which offer native FP4 tensor core support. Direct PTQ to FP4, however, still leaves a noticeable accuracy gap. Recent work on SVDQuant mitigates the errors introduced through FP4 quantization for diffusion models by adding high-precision low-rank corrections obtained via SVD. However, this high-precision low-rank correction requires many bits per rank, and it runs on the same tensor cores as the main FP4 GEMM. We introduce BiLoCo, a low-rank correction method whose left and right factors are stored as binary ${\{\pm 1\}}$ sign vectors rather than as BF16 numbers. The signed format uses $16{\times}$ less storage per rank-one component, and since the correction now consists of additions/subtractions, this can be run on CUDA cores concurrently with the FP4 tensor-core GEMM. On 4B--72B LLMs, BiLoCo matches or improves memory-matched SVDQuant for PTQ. We implement BiLoCo on B300 as a fused single kernel, where the FP4 GEMM runs on tensor cores, the bit-packed correction on the CUDA cores of the same launch, and the two are combined in the GEMM epilogue. With batch size $1$, the fused kernel is at most $11\%$ slower than the FP4-only GEMM for every rank up to $512$. At rank $128$, it is at most $20\%$ slower up to batch size $8$.


### [59] [Anti-coherence Regularized Absolute Scaling for Out-of-Distribution Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32255)
*Donghwan Kim, Youngwoong Jin, Hyunsoo Yoon*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is recognized as a critical problem in AI safety. SCALE is an effective post-hoc OOD detection method in the activation-shaping family that requires no training data and rescales penultimate activations using a percentile-based magnitude ratio. However, this ratio is ill-defined when negative activations are present, since positive and negative contributions cancel within the underlying sum, causing SCALE to degenerate on backbones such as ViT. We address this with an Absolute Fix that computes the ratio over absolute-valued activations rather than discarding negative activations through ReLU. Building on this corrected scale, we propose Anti-coherence regularization, which penalizes the scaling factor by the fraction of a sample's max-logit contributions to the max logit that are sign-mismatched and thereby suppresses unreliable evidence for the predicted class. Despite requiring no access to training data or training labels, our method ties or outperforms activation-shaping methods that rely on training data in terms of AUROC and FPR@95 on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet under the OpenOOD v1.5 protocol.


### [60] [SKG in Practice: OOD Generalization Cases Across FourDomains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32314)
*Jian An, Chaoshi Wu, Xinpan Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This paper presents the engineering implementation methods of the SKG inference framework and provides complete case studies across four domains. Case 1, 2D Dissipative Mechanical System: OOD MSE is 1.69 to 4.12 times lower than the best neural baseline; the state-dependent metric yields a 10^8-order improvement under controlled conditions. Case 2, Target Detection Sim-to-Real: Real-machine mAP increases from 0.2564 to 0.4964 (+24.00 points, single-run experiment, 32 images, point estimate), and AP75 increases from 0.1736 to 0.5765 (+40.29 points), with no real-machine retraining required. Case 3, Sequence Modeling sine to square: OOD MSE is 0.0296 versus Transformer 0.0641 (mean of 5 seeds, p=0.024). Case 4, Molecular Dynamics MD17: ID@100 ADE is 0.0285 versus ESTAG 0.1467; OOD@100 is 0.0316 versus 0.1411 (single seed). For each case, we present the objective, applied SKG theory, parameter settings, baseline comparison protocol, data results, and sources of advantage. Following the cases, we provide the complete methodology: pre-validation, task invariant construction, three-stage training, residual training, and the four theorems and one proposition supporting these methods. This paper not only demonstrates effective configurations of SKG across four domains but also honestly reports the variance of single-run experiments, limitations of single-seed results, negative updates caused by parameterization mismatch, and practical experience of using engineering thresholds as diagnostic signals rather than hard constraints.


### [61] [When to Trust Memory: Retrieval-Guided Probabilistic Spatiotemporal Forecasting under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32329)
*Haochen Lv, Jianhao Zhang, Zhichen Lei, Yang Jiang, Xiaowei Mao, Shengnan Guo, Youfang Lin, Huaiyu Wan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Spatiotemporal forecasting plays an important role in real-world applications, where probabilistic approaches provide uncertainty estimates for decision-making. Real-world systems are inherently non-stationary, leading to distribution shifts between training and test data. However, existing probabilistic methods mainly focus on modeling data distributions under standard settings, while distribution shift methods often handle such shifts in a coarse manner, failing to characterize shift degree and adapt prediction strategy accordingly. As a result, they often produce miscalibrated and unreliable forecasts. To address this issue, we propose REMIND, a retrieval-guided probabilistic framework for spatiotemporal forecasting under distribution shift. REMIND leverages retrieval to estimate how well current observation are supported by historical patterns, enabling adaptive prediction under different shift regimes. It employs spatiotemporal retrieval to construct a memory bank and introduces a dual branch architecture, combining a memory-calibrated probabilistic branch for mild shifts with a deviation-aware probabilistic branch for severe shifts. A shift-adaptive gate, guided by retrieval similarity and memory dispersion, balances the two branches according to shift degree. We further design a hierarchical Gaussian mixture to capture heterogeneous uncertainty over the dual branch predictions, and introduce a memory perturbation training scheme to improve generalization. Experiments on real-world datasets with distribution shifts demonstrate that REMIND consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both forecasting accuracy and probabilistic prediction quality.


### [62] [TENET: Time-point Encoding Network for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32337)
*Dongchan Cho, Juwon Hwang, Keumyeong Kang, Jiho Han, Namsoon Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In unsupervised multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTSAD), local variation can inflate reconstruction and forecasting errors, making observation-space discrepancy an unreliable indicator of abnormal system behavior. Since latent-space scoring can mitigate this limitation, we reframe one-class MTSAD as current-point system-state compatibility estimation and propose TENET, the Time-point Encoding Network for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection. We define the current-point system state as a latent representation of the target time point, constructed from variable-level temporal context in the input window. TENET constructs this state through a temporal layer that retrieves window context relative to the current point, encodes time with current-point-centered embeddings, and adaptively mixes the retrieved context with the current representation. With this design, TENET treats the input window not as the object to reconstruct, forecast, summarize, or score, but as context for constructing a latent representation evaluated with a fixed standard-normal negative log-likelihood score. Across benchmark datasets, TENET achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional experiments further show that TENET remains effective under alternative one-class objectives and outperforms substitutions based on generic sequence encoders, suggesting that its advantage comes from decision-aligned representation construction rather than from a specialized scoring rule. These results indicate that one-class MTSAD can be strong when the latent representation is designed around the current time point.


### [63] [Optimal Multiclass Online Regret with Arbitrary Label Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32339)
*Sajad Ashkezari, Shai Ben-David*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We determine the optimal regret in agnostic multiclass online learning with arbitrary, possibly infinite, label spaces. For every hypothesis class of multiclass Littlestone dimension $d<\infty$, we prove a regret upper bound of $O(\min\{T,\sqrt{dT}\})$ over $T$ rounds, matching the known lower bound. This resolves a question of Hanneke et al. (2023) and extends the optimal binary regret bound of Alon et al. (2021). Our proof strengthens the one-sided expert cover of Hanneke et al. (2023) to a fractional cover. We combine this cover with a minimax reduction to a finite expert game, whose best-response rewards provide the disagreement bounds needed for chaining.


### [64] [Detection and Validation of Hidden Subgroup Disparities in Clinical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32362)
*Alceu Bissoto, Trung-Dung Hoang, Tim Flühmann, Nina Weng, Susu Sun, Christian F. Baumgartner, Lisa M. Koch*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) models used in clinical workflows often perform systematically differently across patient groups, raising concerns about equity and patient safety. Traditional subgroup analyses can expose such disparities only when rich metadata (e.g., patient demographics) is available and captures the sources of performance variation, conditions rarely met in practice. Algorithmic subgroup discovery methods offer a solution by identifying coherent groups from learned feature representations, but because true underlying stratifications are unknown, these methods are hard to evaluate. As a result, subgroup discovery has not been systematically applied or assessed for performance monitoring. Our main objective is to evaluate algorithmic subgroup discovery for performance monitoring, assessing the discovered subgroups' performance gaps and semantic coherence. Investigating cardiomegaly classification in a dataset of 223,228 chest X-rays, we showed that the discovered subgroups exhibited larger performance gaps (accuracies of 0.61-0.90) than traditional metadata-based analysis (0.80-0.94) and were frequently interpretable using patient, acquisition, and model metadata. As a proof of concept, we also showed that annotations from learned subgroups can be used to mitigate the biases they expose. We conclude that subgroup discovery is a practical and effective tool to enhance the performance validation and monitoring of ML systems in healthcare.


### [65] [When Does Dynamics Randomization Test Context Inference?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32414)
*Oleg Smirnov, Sofiane Ennadir, John Pertoft, Bjartur Hjaltason, Sara Karimi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Environments with randomized dynamics are widely used to ask whether world models need explicit context inference. However, randomization can leave little predictive information for a context encoder to add, because distinct latent parameters may produce similar transitions at visited states, or those states may already reveal the active context. We formalize the remaining one-step information as predictive headroom and compare kernels under fixed and visited state--action distributions to distinguish changes in query coverage from changes in context weights. We analyze discrete and continuous environments and find low headroom both when kernels differ little and when visitation reduces an otherwise large discrepancy. At the same time, resetting state components increases headroom, showing that context evidence accumulates in state. We propose reporting this headroom alongside the randomized parameters, so that gains from explicit context inference can be interpreted relative to the information missing from the current state and action.


### [66] [DIGOM: Disentangled Interaction Graphs for Outcome prediction in Multimodal healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32451)
*Hakima Laribi, Olivier Lefebvre, Dan Poenaru, Martin Vallières*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multimodal healthcare data contain relational information that extends beyond individual patient observations. Effective clinical outcome prediction therefore requires not only integrating information from multiple modalities, but also modeling the relationships between them. Standard fusion approaches do not explicitly capture these interactions, while existing graph-based approaches typically model only a subset of them. We propose DIGOM (Disentangled Interaction Graphs for Outcome prediction in Multimodal healthcare), a graph-based framework that jointly models three complementary interaction types: Intra-patient cross-modal, Cross-patient same-modal, and Cross-patient cross-modal interactions. DIGOM learns a sparse multiplex graph with disentangled views, each encoding interactions between pairs of modalities across patients. Discrete adjacency matrices with patient-adaptive connectivity are sampled via a differentiable mechanism, enabling end-to-end learning and allowing the model to select informative relationships without imposing a fixed number of neighbors. A regularization objective maintains overall graph sparsity, while message passing integrates this relational context into patient representations for outcome prediction. We evaluate DIGOM on two multimodal healthcare datasets, MIMIC-IV and NIH-TB, across five clinical prediction tasks. DIGOM achieves the highest AUROC on all five tasks while using fewer connections than existing multi-interaction approaches. Ablation studies reveal that each interaction type's contribution varies across tasks, highlighting the need to adapt relational structures per task. DIGOM also demonstrates greater robustness to missing modalities, suggesting learned relationships provide complementary information when modalities are unavailable. Post-hoc analyses further reveal task- and outcome-specific connectivity patterns in the learned graphs.


### [67] [DualGSL: Prior-Guided Graph Structure Learning with Node-Adaptive Prediction Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32462)
*Yuxia Sun, Yutszyuk Wong, Huihong Chen, Longhao Lv, Chang-Dong Wang, Li Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph structure learning (GSL) adapts propagation graphs to prediction tasks, but the relative predictive value of feature-derived priors and learned relations can vary across nodes. We propose DualGSL, a prior-guided framework for transductive node classification that learns node-specific weights for combining predictions from separate prior and task-adaptive propagation paths. The prior branch augments a feature graph with virtual class nodes to incorporate training-label information, while the structural branch combines learned affinity graphs with the feature-graph prior. The two branches share encoder parameters while preserving distinct representations and classification logits. A node-adaptive gate fuses the branch logits based on their projected representations, element-wise absolute difference, and prediction confidence. A joint objective integrates fused and branch-specific classification losses with prior-branch class-anchor alignment, coordinating graph construction, representation learning, and prediction fusion. Experiments against seven baselines on the AMG18 malware attribution dataset and the widely used 20News text classification and USPS handwritten-digit recognition benchmarks show that DualGSL achieves the highest cross-dataset average accuracy and Macro-F1 among the evaluated methods. Ablation studies on AMG18 further demonstrate the benefit of combining prior and learned propagation through logit fusion.


### [68] [Sharpness-aware Orthogonalized Momentum Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32465)
*Geetika, Shivani Singh Chauhan, Gauranshi Gupta, Anant Jain, Kaushik Kalyanaraman, Bapi Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Adversarial sharpness penalty per iteration of a base optimizer, known as sharpness-aware minimization (SAM), is an accepted technique to improve generalization in deep learning. Recently, Muon has emerged as a dominant optimization algorithm for training large language models (LLMs). It achieves lower sample complexity for heavy-tailed gradient updates - a phenomenon common in LLM training. However, sharpness penalties with Muon updates are yet largely unexplored in the literature. Moreover, the behavior of SAM under heavy-tailed noise is unknown both theoretically and empirically. A potential reason for this gap is the additional cost of extra gradient computation per iteration for penalty perturbations in SAM, which becomes prohibitive for LLMs. This paper introduces Sharpness-aware Orthogonalized Momentum Algorithm (SOMA) to combine a computationally efficient and theoretically grounded perturbation scheme with the Muon algorithm. The key idea is to use stale (purely) stochastic gradient noise for adversarial perturbations. Theoretically, we show that SOMA's sample complexity is invariant to the perturbation direction. We provide explorations and insights into SOMA's robustness to heavy-tailed gradient noise. We extensively explore SOMA's efficacy in (a) mitigating quality degradation due to post-training quantization, and (b) catastrophic forgetting during fine-tuning of LLMs.


### [69] [Online Continual Learning in Edge-Cloud Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32472)
*Charalampos Davalas, Dimitrios Michail, Christos Diou, Iraklis Varlamis, Konstantinos Tserpes*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deployed machine learning models in real-world IoT and edge/cloud systems face a critical reliability bottleneck: the requirement for online adaptation to non-stationary data streams while operating without access to the full original training dataset. Performance is severely degraded when models encounter Out-of-Distribution (OOD) inputs or suffer from catastrophic forgetting. Addressing these challenges requires adaptation mechanisms capable of learning from evolving data streams while preserving previously acquired knowledge. This paper proposes an edge-cloud methodology for integrating Continual Learning (CL) with Out-of-Distribution (OOD) Detection and Concept Drift Detection in IoT applications. The methodology incorporates ODIN and ECDD as alternative mechanisms for identifying informative samples for annotation and triggering rehearsal-based model updates. Experimental results across automotive, healthcare, and agricultural datasets demonstrate the applicability of the proposed methodology and its potential to support model adaptation while mitigating catastrophic forgetting.


### [70] [A curated multi-endpoint drug-protein bioactivity dataset with construct-aware protein targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32490)
*Lina Abou-Abbas, Nour Mhanna, Hasan Bazzi, Sleiman El Hajj, Abdulkader Helwan, Rabah Omrani, Khadidja Henni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Public drug-protein bioactivity resources commonly identify targets by UniProt accession, although one accession may represent distinct experimental sequences. We present Curate-DTI, a construct-aware dataset derived from the 1 July 2026 BindingDB export, with IC50, Ki, Kd and EC50 processed independently. The release contains 2,919,717 measurements, 1,291,872 standardized ligands, 9,668 sequence-defined protein entities and 2,275,683 endpoint-specific interactions spanning 8,336 reviewed UniProt accessions. Protein entities combine the accession with the reported experimental sequence, preserving constructs that accession-only processing would merge. Across the four endpoints, 4,204 endpoint-specific ligand-accession groups showed different classification labels across sequence-defined protein entities. Exact and inequality-qualified observations provide relation-aware classification evidence, whereas regression targets use exact values only. Connected Parquet tables preserve individual measurements, standardized structures, protein sequences, aggregated interactions and available assay and source provenance. The resource supports endpoint-specific classification and regression while allowing users to select molecular representations, protein representations and evaluation splits. Preprocessing code, a column dictionary, exclusion reports and validation summaries accompany the release.


### [71] [Accordion: Elastic-Depth Inference via Fixed-Prefix Layer Looping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32492)
*Yudong Zhang, Wei Ding, Kaili Zheng, Yuchen Ding, Zhenyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models serve applications with diverse inference budgets, but training and maintaining separate models for each budget incurs substantial costs. Recent Looped Transformers enable flexible inference computation through parameter reuse, but maintaining strong performance across different inference budgets remains an open challenge. We introduce Accordion, a mixture-of-experts-based model that supports five inference budgets through fixed-prefix layer looping. Each budget follows a pre-defined execution path, while the minimum budget retains the unlooped model. Joint pretraining samples one budget per micro-batch and optimizes its next-token prediction loss without additional cross-budget consistency objectives. We further preserve these inference budgets through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, where parameter-group merging and budget-matched multi-teacher on-policy distillation integrate three domain experts into one model. After 3.6T tokens of pretraining, increasing inference depth improves Accordion-6B-A0.5B's five-category macro-average across 37 tasks by 3.01 percentage points, with the middle budget capturing 89% of this gain using half as many additional layer executions. The final model surpasses each domain expert on its corresponding task-and-budget average, while achieving strong instruction-following performance across all budgets and retaining depth-dependent gains in mathematical reasoning and code generation.


### [72] [DRIVE: Fine-tuning via Data Contribution- and Diversity-aware Weighting with Prior Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32501)
*Qing Liu, Xinrui Chen, Yi Du, Ou Wu, Weiyao Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Single-stage fine-tuning is pervasive, yet catastrophic forgetting persists when the source pre-training data is inaccessible. Data weighting affords a natural lever to modulate sample influence. Yet existing weighting methods lack a principled mechanism to explicitly arbitrate knowledge retention against new-task adaptation, and provide no unified differentiable objective to accommodate auxiliary signals such as sample diversity and sample characteristics. This is because current schemes privilege one goal over the other, and no explicit gradient-compatible encoding exists for such signals. To address this, we propose DRIVE, a principled and efficient weight assignment framework. It derives per-sample weights via a unified differentiable objective where three coupled signals co-evolve: Shapley additivity splits contribution into anti-forgetting and adaptation components, which are reshaped by a covariance-based diversity term and stabilized by a sample-characteristic prior. Far from a mere combination, this yields an end-to-end gradient-based scheme balancing stability against plasticity. Theoretically, we prove that DRIVE enjoys superior stability-plasticity guarantees over existing weighting schemes and enhances parameter estimation robustness. Empirically, it attains superior overall performance, faster convergence, and stronger robustness. Code at this https URL .


### [73] [BAARC: Uncertainty-Driven Adaptive Partial Spectral Measurement to Full Reconstruction for Material Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32505)
*Adithya Ashok Chalain Valapil, Aishwarya Venkataramanan, Joachim Denzler*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Spectroscopic instruments with limited wavenumber coverage require accurate scan positioning or multiple measurements to complete the material analysis. This often requires prerequisite knowledge about the material under diagnosis to measure the spectral regions with distinguishing peaks. In our study, we simulate a Bayesian Adaptive Acquisition with Reconstruction-driven Classification (BAARC) framework for Raman spectral measurements, using masked training to learn spectral features that dynamically drive laser channel selection until the material is identified. We present a jointly trained two-level framework that takes progressive partial measurements of small wavenumber patches across the complete spectral range and, at each step, first attempts to reconstruct the full spectra until the model uncertainty is low. Secondly, material classification is performed at each reconstruction step until stoppage. Uncertainty quantification during reconstruction steps helps determine the measurement position of our next patch, while combining it with classification uncertainty determines measurement termination. By closing the loop between hardware acquisition and deep probabilistic modeling, the proposed system minimizes physical measurement steps while maintaining both spectral fidelity and diagnostic accuracy. Experimental evaluations demonstrate that our framework achieves high-accuracy sample identification using only a fraction of full-spectrum channels, offering a scalable solution for real-time, high-throughput spectroscopic instruments.


### [74] [Amortized Thompson Sampling and Efficient Information-Directed Sampling for Simulator-Based Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32514)
*Michal Kobiela, Michael U. Gutmann*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Thompson sampling is a widely used strategy for sequential decision-making under uncertainty, offering a principled way to balance exploration and exploitation. This strategy involves maximizing a target objective under a single sample drawn from the current belief over unknown quantities. Conventionally, this optimization is performed once the belief has been inferred from data, and it becomes computationally burdensome when many Thompson samples are required, especially in continuous action spaces. In this work, we introduce an approach that amortizes this process ahead of time, before any data is collected, so that an approximation of the full Thompson sampling distribution is available immediately once observations arrive. We employ this approach to solve the multi-round setting, involving sequential data acquisition and Thompson-sample recommendation across rounds. Beyond accelerating Thompson sampling, our approach enables efficient estimation of expected information gain about the optimal action. This can be used to select more informative Thompson samples or to obtain maximally informative designs with optimization. Combining this estimate of expected information gain with immediate regret supports information-directed sampling (IDS), which can outperform Thompson sampling by explicitly balancing these two quantities. We demonstrate our approach on two simulator-based design problems in synthetic biology, where the simulators have intractable likelihoods.


### [75] [Principled Nonlinear Concept Erasure and Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32516)
*Idan Simai, Shauli Ravfogel, Uri Shaham*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Learned representations often encode sensitive attributes, such as gender or race, that may be undesirable or unsafe to retain. Concept erasure aims to remove this information while preserving task-relevant content. Existing methods remain incomplete: linear methods provide guarantees only against linear probes, while nonlinear methods achieve stronger empirical erasure but lack formal guarantees and often sacrifice utility. In this work, we introduce a principled conditional autoencoder framework for nonlinear concept erasure. We follow the principle that erasure should remove only the sensitive attribute and nothing else, and we show that, under the stated assumptions, perfect reconstruction together with population-level independence is sufficient for perfect nonlinear guardedness, with preservation guarantees in the imperfect-reconstruction regime. Empirically, we attain a superior utility-erasure trade-off across multiple datasets, sensitive attributes, and language models, surpassing even the supervised variants of competing methods despite using no target-task labels, while being simpler to optimize and converging substantially faster. Beyond erasure, our framework yields concept conversion (representation steering) as a by-product: by changing only the target concept at inference time, it produces representations corresponding to different concept values - at no additional training cost - a capability no prior erasure method provides, and on which we likewise demonstrate strong empirical performance.


### [76] [Shared Is Not Enough: Predictor Width and Factor Readability Under Masked Latent Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32562)
*Nilotpal Kakati*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Masked latent prediction methods such as I-JEPA aim to learn predictable features, but what determines which information they retain is less clear. We built a simulator in which every factor behind an image is known, so a probe can measure how readable each factor remains after training. We trained small encoders (0.7M parameters) with I-JEPA, SimCLR and BYOL on the same pairs of views and probed a factor that both views share. I-JEPA had a predictor with half the encoder's width and depth, the proportions it uses for ViT-B. Its median score was about 40% of SimCLR's. Widening the predictor to the encoder's width reversed this ordering at every training seed, against both other methods. We release the simulator and the trained models.


### [77] [From Parameter Rebasin to Functional Transfer Across Heterogeneous Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32581)
*Filippo Rinaldi, Aniello Panariello, Giacomo Salici, Simone Magistri, Angelo Porrello, Andrew D. Bagdanov, Simone Calderara*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Adapting pretrained Transformers to downstream tasks produces task-specific updates that are expensive to reproduce for every model variant. Reusing these updates across different depths and widths is challenging because their parameters have incompatible shapes and do not share a canonical coordinate system. We introduce Ariadne, a training-free method that transfers task-specific behavior across heterogeneous Transformers without resizing the source model or transporting its parameters. Rather than treating the source task vector, i.e. the difference in parameter space between the fine-tuned and the base models, as the object to move, Ariadne characterizes fine-tuning by the activation changes it induces at matched blocks. First, it aligns these effects to a target base through local Procrustes maps. Then, it directly synthesizes a native target update by closed-form ridge regression. Across Transformer expansion, reduction and language-model transfer, Ariadne improves target-model performance while substantially reducing the time and memory required to construct an update relative to previous cross-architecture rebasin approaches. These results show that local functional effects provide an efficient alternative to parameter transport.


### [78] [Contextual Decision Making: Predictive or Generative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32584)
*L. Jeff Hong, Weihuan Huang, Xinyao Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Contextual decision making uses observed covariates to guide decisions under uncertainty. Two natural approaches are predict-then-optimize (PtO), which learns the conditional expected objective, and generate-then-optimize (GtO), which learns conditional uncertainty and optimizes using generated scenarios. We study when each approach is preferable by examining how the choice of learning target affects statistical accuracy and optimization tractability. Although PtO learns a narrower target, our regret bounds show how differences in smoothness can offset GtO's additional dimensional burden. Exploiting smoothness also raises an optimization tradeoff: within the class of nonnegative-weight PtO methods studied, a lower bound limits the gains from additional smoothness, while more flexible methods may introduce signed weights and lose the guarantee of preserving convexity. GtO preserves scenario-wise convexity, but requires learning a richer object and generating scenarios for optimization. Synthetic experiments and a real case study illustrate how these tradeoffs vary with data availability and problem structure. Together with the scope for reusing a model across tasks, our findings provide practical guidance for choosing between the approaches without identifying a universal winner.


### [79] [Trajectory Matching: Discrete Flow Matching with Structured Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32602)
*Niklas Kemper, Marten Lienen, David Lüdke, Stephan Günnemann*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The natural units of structured discrete generation are often not independent single-component edits but richer actions: attaching a fragment to a molecule, inserting a node together with an incident edge, or growing a hierarchy by adding a child. Yet most existing discrete flow matching methods (DFM) are restricted to independent component-wise edits over fixed-size states. This restriction stems from endpoint conditioning: endpoints fix the source and target during training but not the action sequence between them, making tractable conditional rates hard to define for complex actions that cannot be factorized component-wise. We propose trajectory matching, which conditions the flow on a full sequence of actions connecting the endpoints. This yields principled, simulation-free training targets for user-defined action spaces, including actions that change state size, modify multiple components, or depend on earlier actions. We prove that the resulting objective recovers the marginal flow and that trajectory matching strictly subsumes factorized DFM, while additionally capturing actions with prerequisite structure such as hierarchy growth or fragment-based molecule generation. Empirically, on tree and molecular graph generation, trajectory matching with domain-aligned actions matches or surpasses factorized DFM baselines (lifting MOSES validity from 92.8% to 99.2%) and transfers without retraining to substructure extension and linking. Trajectory matching enlarges the design space of discrete flow matching by turning the action vocabulary into a modeling choice. An interactive explanation of trajectory matching is available at this https URL .


### [80] [Interpreting Supervised Pairwise Graph Representations: Local Evidence, Confounding, and Provable Post-hoc Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32611)
*Ilya Brekin, Ariela Moldavskaya, Elina Sidorova, Ural Valeev*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Supervised pairwise graph representations convert pairwise predictive relations into sample-specific graphs, but graph-level accuracy does not by itself show that higher-order topology carries the signal. In a controlled Transformer error-detection task, supervised pairwise graphs outperform attention-as-adjacency (ROC-AUC $0.839$ versus $0.559$; the comparable no-confidence pair is $0.687$ versus $0.559$), but their apparent advantage over model confidence disappears after controlling for sequence length, which is almost perfectly recoverable from the pooled hidden state ($R^2=0.9995$). On published HCP Emotion ensemble graphs, signed node aggregation already acts as an ensemble of supervised pairwise predictions. Edge magnitude alone is near chance; topology-aware augmentations change ROC-AUC by at most $0.004$, with only sign-separated degree showing small positive changes across all tested graph sizes, while a validation-tuned three-layer GCN provides no measurable improvement over the signed node-summary baseline. A Fisher post-hoc corrector satisfies its distribution-agnostic guarantees across the evaluated feature representations and remains stable under reduced labelled budgets; in a near-error-free fMRI calibration regime the bound correctly becomes statistically uninformative. A separate cross-attention analysis identifies a redundant reversal circuit and downstream EOS-timing and copy failures. Higher-order topology should therefore be credited only after local supervised evidence and recoverable nuisance variables have been excluded.


### [81] [Earth embeddings are decomposable records of the land surface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32617)
*Robin Young, Srinivasan Keshav*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Ecological monitoring, carbon accounting and biodiversity assessment increasingly rely on annual Earth embeddings--learned vectors that compress a year of satellite observation for every ten-meter pixel on Earth, consumed in place of the imagery itself. What these vectors mean has not been established. Here we decompose two widely used embedding products and show that they contain individual ecological features, including fire, forest clearing and crop type, validated against independent reference data on events across six continents. The recovered concepts date abrupt canopy loss to the first post-disturbance growing season and resolve severity well enough to separate prescribed fire from wildfire. Small fires that recover quickly are detectable in one product but not the other, which instead separates crops more strongly.


### [82] [The Standard Deviation in VICReg Buys a Safety Margin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32630)
*Hawar Dizayee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: VICReg prevents collapse with a hinge on the standard deviation of each embedding dimension, and its authors argue from the gradient that a hinge on the variance would fail. To our knowledge, this claim has not been tested experimentally. We test it in a controlled setting (ResNet-18, CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100), changing only the hinge and sweeping its weight $\mu$ over five orders of magnitude. At the default weight ($\mu = 1$), the two hinges reach the same linear-probe accuracy on CIFAR-10 (about 89.5%). Both collapse below a threshold weight, but the standard-deviation hinge's threshold is 35-130$\times$ lower. CIFAR-100 shows a consistent gap. In training, the variance hinge's gradient fades as the feature variance shrinks, while the standard-deviation hinge's does not until that variance is very small. The standard-deviation hinge therefore buys a safety margin: it keeps training at weights far below the point where the variance hinge collapses.


### [83] [WL-SeqVAE: Sequence-Based Graph Generation Using Variational Autoencoders and Weisfeiler-Leman Colors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32650)
*Iakovos Evdaimon, Giannis Nikolentzos, Achilleas Tsimichodimos, Christos Xypolopoulos, Michalis Vazirgiannis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce WL-SeqVAE, a sequence-based variational autoencoder for generating graphs. The key observation behind WL-SeqVAE is that node colors induced by the Weisfeiler-Leman (WL) algorithm provide compact discrete summaries of local graph structure and can therefore serve as structural tokens for generation. Rather than learning a distribution over adjacency matrices directly, our method maps each graph to a sequence of WL colors and employs a variational autoencoder that encodes this sequence into a Gaussian latent distribution, from which an autoregressive decoder reconstructs the graph. During generation, compatibility constraints derived from the WL colors guide node and edge predictions, combining global information from the latent representation with local structural consistency. This formulation turns graph generation into a sequence generation problem and avoids directly parameterizing a quadratic adjacency matrix. We evaluate WL-SeqVAE on molecular and general graph generation tasks. Across the evaluated datasets, WL-SeqVAE is competitive with established baselines on several local structural and molecular quality metrics. Code is available at this https URL


### [84] [DT-JEPA: Learning to Predict Future Representations for Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32657)
*Changxu Ni, Yiyang Ni, Jun Li, Feiran You, Hongyang Du, Haitao Zhao, Shi Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Decision Transformer (DT) has demonstrated strong performance in various offline reinforcement learning (RL) tasks by conditioning action generation on expected future returns and historical trajectories. However, treating expected future returns as the condition provides an incomplete description of the future, as trajectories with different state transitions and behaviors may share similar returns. Moreover, expected future returns inherently depend on task-specific reward functions, which makes policy learning reliant on reward-labeled datasets and restricts its scalability to tasks with similar but unseen rewards. Recently, Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA) have emerged as a promising paradigm of self-supervised learning-based world models, which learn predictive representations of future dynamics in latent space rather than reconstructing future states in the original space. Inspired by JEPA, we propose DT-JEPA, an offline pretraining and online fine-tuning framework that learns general knowledge from reward-free offline trajectories and achieves excellent adaptation to unseen tasks. Instead of predicting a single deterministic future embedding, we design a novel probabilistic predictor to model the conditional distribution of future representations in the latent space, thereby capturing both predictive information and uncertainty about future dynamics. Theoretically, we show that 1) the JEPA objective seeks to maximize a variational lower bound on the mutual information between historical representations and future trajectories; and 2) predictive future-conditioned policy learning admits a finite-sample performance bound with an O(N-1/4) estimation term. Experiments on D4RL benchmarks demonstrate that DT-JEPA achieves efficient adaptation across varying offline RL tasks, outperforming traditional DT-based and other offline RL methods.


### [85] [PlayerEmbed: Learning Behavioral Fingerprint Representations from Raw Input Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32727)
*Tobias Schneider, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Priya Tomar, Shahzeb Qamar, Tobias Deußer, Rafet Sifa*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Raw player input sequences in video games contain fine-grained behavior, but are high-dimensional and noisy, which makes large-scale analytics challenging. To address this, we introduce PlayerEmbed, a framework that learns compact and discriminative player embeddings using deep metric learning. Our approach uses a hybrid CNN-LSTM encoder trained on raw input sequences from 1,000 players. We evaluate the model on held-out sequences from these players and on sequences from 1,000 players entirely unseen during training to assess generalization to unseen identities. For player identification, a nearest-centroid classifier built from reference embeddings achieves strong performance, reaching balanced accuracies of 0.950 for players seen during training and 0.879 for players unseen during training. Systematically increasing the number of training players substantially improves generalization to unseen players, indicating a transition from simple fingerprinting to learning transferable behavioral patterns.


### [86] [Internal Discrepancy for Contrastive Learning: Generalization Bounds and Downstream Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32729)
*Jialiang Wang, Qing Ma, Xiong Zhou, Gangfeng Hu, Haoliang Li, Xianming Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Contrastive learning has emerged as a popular paradigm for training representation models. While prior studies pursue more practical and less restrictive assumptions to enhance theoretical understanding of contrastive learning, they essentially describe the interconnections among representations within the data or induced by the function class. To encapsulate these assumptions, we propose the measure of internal discrepancy that quantifies the difference between the mean representation of positive samples and latent cluster centers. We then prove an internal discrepancy-aware generalization bound for downstream supervised tasks following contrastive learning, without requiring additional assumptions. This generalization bound not only serves as a unifying framework connecting various assumptions but also motivates us to further reduce the internal discrepancy, thereby introducing the relaxed k-means loss as an approximation. Moreover, we investigate the downstream behaviors of contrastive learning from the perspective of contrastive learning-incurred supervised losses. In the presence of class imbalance, we shed light on why contrastive learning can facilitate imbalanced learning. Finally, we investigate the optimality of these incurred supervised losses, proving several intriguing properties.


### [87] [Strongly Adaptive Online Learning with Time-Varying Movement Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32740)
*Andrew Jacobsen, Emmanuel Esposito, Hao Qiu, Mengxiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study strongly adaptive regret for Online Convex Optimization (OCO) with time-varying movement costs, a setting that evaluates a learner's performance over arbitrary sub-intervals. This framework captures complex sequential decision-making problems where both the environment and the cost of switching decisions fluctuate over time. We propose a novel algorithm which, for any interval $[a,b] \subseteq [T]$, achieves a data-dependent regret bound of $ \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left( \sqrt{(D^2+DP_{[a,b]})\sum_{t \in [a,b]}(\|g_t\|^2+\lambda_t\|g_t\|)} \right) $, where $D$ is the diameter of the decision domain, $P_{[a,b]}$ is the path length of the comparator sequence over $[a,b]$, $g_t$ is the gradient of the loss, and $\lambda_t$ is the movement-cost coefficient at round $t$. Key to our approach is a novel reduction to sleeping experts with movement costs, a previously unexplored variant that is of independent interest. As a result of this development, we further establish the first strongly-adaptive guarantees under delayed feedback. Our approach also naturally extends to OCO with time-varying memory, where we obtain strongly-adaptive guarantees which improve the best-known bounds.


### [88] [On the Identifiability of Hybrid Deep Generative Models: Meta-Learning as a Solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32748)
*Yubo Ye, Maryam Toloubidokhti, Sumeet Vadhavkar, Xiajun Jiang, Huafeng Liu, Linwei Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The interest in leveraging physics-based inductive bias in deep learning has resulted in recent development of hybrid deep generative models (hybrid-DGMs) that integrate known physics-based mathematical expressions in neural generative models. To identify these hybrid-DGMs requires inferring parameters of the physics-based component along with their neural component. The identifiability of these hybrid-DGMs, however, has not yet been theoretically probed or established. How does the existing theory of the un-identifiability of general DGMs apply to hybrid-DGMs? What may be an effective approach to construct a hybrid-DGM with theoretically-proven identifiability? This paper provides the first theoretical probe into the identifiability of hybrid-DGMs, and presents meta-learning as a novel solution to construct identifiable hybrid-DGMs. On synthetic and real-data benchmarks, we provide strong empirical evidence for the un-identifiability of existing hybrid-DGMs using unconditional priors, and strong identifiability results of the presented meta-formulations of hybrid-DGMs. Code is available at this https URL .


### [89] [Direction-Aware Token Credit Assignment with Reward-Model Gradients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32772)
*Yikuan Hu, Jifeng Zhu, Minle Su, Jiaqin Yue, Gujie Shao, Yanzhe Chen, Haoxuan Jia, Haoran Xu, Wangzhou Dai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: GRPO shares a response-level advantage across tokens, leaving unresolved how strongly each token should contribute to learning. Updates induced by the shared advantage reinforce or suppress the sampled token at each position, but these updates do not always point toward increasing reward at the token level. This motivates a work-based view of credit: just as work depends on the component of force along a displacement, token credit should reflect the local reward change along the policy update direction. We introduce Direction-Aware Token Credit Assignment (DATC), which maps frozen reward-model gradients into policy logit space and allocates weights by evaluating the alignment between the reward direction and GRPO's signed sampled-action update direction, while preserving the sign and mean of the response-level advantage. DATC outperforms GRPO in all four settings across two model families.


### [90] [Deep Learning-Based Prediction of HER2 Status from Whole-Brain MRI in Breast Cancer Brain Metastases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32774)
*Sara Early, Chris Kang, Vibujithan Vigneshwaran, Kimberly Amador, Roger Y. Tsang, Nils D. Forkert*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Purpose: Human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) status is a key biomarker relevant for prognostication and treatment decision making in metastatic breast cancer. Thus, the aim of this work was to explore whether HER2 status can be predicted directly from whole-brain post-contrast T1-weighted (T1w) MRI scans in breast cancer brain metastases (BCBM) using deep learning, and whether providing explicit tumour segmentation information improves prediction. Methods: Using the publicly available BCBM-Radiogenomics dataset, we analysed 135 patients, including 70 HER2+ and 65 HER2- patients. Three approaches were implemented and evaluated: a conventional radiomics model, a simple fully convolutional network (SFCN) using T1w MRI scans alone, and an SFCN using T1w MRI scans with an expert-derived tumour mask as an additional input channel. Results: The radiomics model achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.602 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.528--0.677) while the SFCN achieved a ROC-AUC of 0.667 (95% CI, 0.63--0.71) using T1w MRI scans alone, which increased to 0.692 (95% CI, 0.58--0.81) when an explicit tumour mask was additionally provided. Conclusion: However, the modest performance across all approaches demonstrates the difficulty of predicting HER2 status from structural MRI scans alone. The difference between the segmentation-free and mask-guided SFCN models suggests that much of the predictive signal captured by the deep-learning model may be learned directly from whole-brain T1w MRI without requiring explicit tumour delineation. These findings support further investigation of segmentation-free deep-learning approaches as a potential non-invasive strategy to support HER2 status determination in BCBM.


### [91] [CARES: Coverage-Aware Replay Exemplar Selection for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32799)
*Arundhati S. Shanbhag, Brian B. Moser, Federico Raue, Tobias C. Nauen, Andreas Dengel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Replay-based Continual Learning mitigates catastrophic forgetting by keeping a buffer of past samples, making buffer composition critical under a fixed memory budget. Recent coreset-based replay methods improve buffer quality using model-derived gradients, losses, or auxiliary optimization, but incur substantial computational overhead as memory is repeatedly updated. We propose CARES, a continual model-optimization-free approach for buffer construction that instead exploits the geometry of a frozen pretrained feature space using class-aware, density-weighted coverage. We evaluate CARES across balanced, imbalanced, and label-noisy streams under online class- and task-incremental settings. On Tiny-ImageNet, CARES is 2.3 to 8.8 times faster than existing coreset-based selectors. Although CARES achieves this speedup using no training signal from the continual learner, it ranks among the state of the art across balanced, imbalanced, and label-noisy streams under online class- and task-incremental settings, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 7.58 percentage points under class imbalance while also attaining higher average accuracy on Tiny-ImageNet. Code will be released upon publication


### [92] [Multiplicative vs. Additive State Coupling in Learned Optimizer Schedules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32801)
*M. Harper Langston, Pierre-David Letourneau, Dalton Jones, Roland Memisevic, Peter Scott, Mingu Lee, Richard A. Lethin, Christopher Lott, Fatih Porikli, Harris Teague*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We learn schedules for a continuous penalty relaxation of MAX-CUT by backpropagating through an unrolled classical solver. A state-blind schedule generalizes to instances substantially larger than those used for training. A state-adaptive bilinear schedule also generalizes out of distribution, outperforming its untrained baseline across 15 seeds ($t=4.1$). By contrast, an additive ablation with identical inputs and output heads is statistically indistinguishable from the bilinear model in distribution but performs worse than its own baseline out of distribution ($t=-11.7$). This separation appears without regularization and persists across a $30$ round weight-decay sweep: the bilinear advantage remains significant at every tested value, whereas the additive model never recovers. The results in this setting isolate how solver state enters the schedule, rather than state adaptivity alone, as the architectural distinction associated with out-of-distribution transfer.


### [93] [U-NeXt: General-purpose event detection for neonatal EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32811)
*Robert Hogan, Sean R. Mathieson, Aurel Luca, Soraia Ventura, Sean Griffin, Geraldine B. Boylan, John M. O'Toole*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We developed U-NeXt, a general framework for neonatal electroencephalography (EEG) event detection based on semantic segmentation. Its convolutional encoder-decoder directly segments individual channels using long input segments to preserve temporal context. A shared architecture and training framework were developed using low-voltage interval, artefact, and seizure datasets containing 51, 98, and 278 neonates, respectively, comprising over 11,000 hours of EEG and 82,000 annotated events. Performance was compared with task-specific ConvNeXt classifiers. For seizure detection, we also evaluated U-NeXt+, trained on an expanded dataset. Both U-NeXt variants and the ConvNeXt baseline were evaluated on two independent held-out seizure datasets, Helsinki and Cork, comprising 130 neonates, with recordings annotated by three experts. U-NeXt improved detection across all three event types, with particular gains in sensitivity for short events. U-NeXt+ achieved Matthews correlation coefficients of 0.862 and 0.891 on Helsinki and Cork, respectively, and improved hourly seizure burden correlations by 30-40% relative to ConvNeXt. U-NeXt+ met the criterion for expert-level performance on both datasets (p < 0.001). In separate computational benchmarks, U-NeXt reduced batch inference time relative to ConvNeXt by 254-fold on a central processing unit and 200-fold on a graphics processing unit. A common segmentation framework improves detection across diverse neonatal EEG event types while substantially reducing inference time. Accurate seizure burden estimates and performance comparable to expert reviewers support its potential use in continuous monitoring and clinical decision support.


### [94] [On the Limits of Sparse Linear Model with Shuffled Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32822)
*Yan Zhang, Jiong Tuo, Meng Ding, Yuheng Ma, Di Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Sparse linear regression is a fundamental problem in high-dimensional statistics, and its private counterparts leave a large gap between the central and the local model of differential privacy. Shuffled differential privacy (SDP) sits between the two, offering accuracy closer to the central model without the trust requirement of a central curator. This paper studies sparse linear regression under both non-interactive and interactive SDP protocols. In the non-interactive setting, any non-interactive $(\epsilon,\delta)$-SDP protocol is shown to incur an $\ell_2$-norm estimation error of $\Omega( \frac{\sqrt{dk}}{n\epsilon} )$ with dimension $d$, sparsity $k$, and $n$ users, revealing an inherent dimensionality barrier. In addition, a non-interactive algorithm is proposed that achieves an $\ell_2$-norm upper bound of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}( \sqrt{\frac{k}{n}} + \frac{d\sqrt{k}}{n\epsilon})$. To overcome this barrier, we turn to the interactive user-level setting, where each user holds $m$ samples. A two-round interactive algorithm is developed that first recovers the support through a heavy hitter step and then estimates the parameter, achieving an error of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}( \sqrt{\frac{k}{nm}} + \frac{k}{n\sqrt{m}\epsilon} )$ with no polynomial dependence on $d$. Furthermore, by extending the score attack framework to user-level data, a new lower bound $\Omega( \sqrt{\frac{k\log(d/k)}{nm}} + \frac{k\log(d/k)}{n\sqrt{m}\epsilon} )$ is established for any user-level $(\epsilon,\delta)$-DP, showing that the proposed algorithm is near-optimal with only a gap of the factor $\log(d/k)$.


### [95] [Neural LoFi with Backward Coupling: A Spectral Theory of Cross-Layer Feature Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32849)
*Yatin Dandi, Matteo Vilucchio, Hugo Tabanelli, Florent Krzakala*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: During joint training, backpropagation allows later layers to reshape earlier representations, a feedback mechanism absent from purely layer-wise learning. We extend Neural Low-Degree Filtering (Neural LoFi), a recently proposed layer-wise spectral surrogate for feature learning, to capture this interaction. Starting from the exact backpropagation gradients, we derive a local coupling between a neuron's incoming weights and its corresponding next-layer column. In a controlled small-initialization regime with smooth activations and fixed surroundings, these dynamics amplify singular modes of a cross-layer correlation operator. Each growing mode pairs an earlier feature with an upper-layer response through shared neuron coefficients. This motivates a backward-coupling algorithm in which upper-layer sensitivities generate sample-dependent effective labels for earlier spectral steps, while corrected features refine upward transmission. A function-space variational principle characterizes the selected feature-response pairs by maximizing the label correlation of their product under kernel-norm constraints. For three-layer networks, we derive self-consistent continuous-time dynamics for the filtered hidden-layer features, the activation kernels, and the network output under the proposed backward coupling. In a hierarchical multi-index model, we establish a feature-recovery separation: backward coupling recovers lower-layer directions invisible to the original forward label-weighted statistic. The linear specialization recovers residual-dependent singular-mode dynamics, while paired modes provide a dynamical mechanism for linked low-rank covariance components consistent with the rainbow-network picture. Experiments with fully connected and convolutional networks on binary CIFAR-10 show improved accuracy over forward Neural LoFi at matched widths.


### [96] [Near-Optimal List Replicability from Littlestone Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32853)
*Ari Blondal, Pooya Hatami, Chavdar Lalov, Sivan Tretiak*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the list replicability number in the realizable PAC learning framework and prove that every concept class $H$ over a countable domain with Littlestone dimension $d$ and dual VC dimension $v^*$ satisfies \[ \mathrm{LR}(H)\leq 2^{O(d\min\{\log d,v^*\})}. \] This matches the worst-case lower bound of $2^{\Omega(d)}$ up to a logarithmic factor in the exponent and exponentially improves the previous best upper bound $\mathrm{LR}(H)\le 2^{2^{O(d)}}$ of Bun, Livni, and Moran [FOCS 2020]. Unlike earlier bounds of $2^{O_\epsilon(d^2)}$ for fixed accuracy $\epsilon>0$ implied by the work of Ghazi, Golowich, Kumar, and Manurangsi [STOC 2021] and Lyu [STOC 2026], our bound is independent of $\epsilon$. The $2^{O(d\log d)}$ bound follows from a new inductive framework for bounding list replicability that builds on the recent notion of irreducibility from differentially private learning. The $2^{O(dv^*)}$ bound follows from a simple globally stable learner that takes the majority vote of multiple empirical risk minimizers, with stability established using tools from stable regularity.


### [97] [Regularised RL improves sim-to-real transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32855)
*Danilo Vucetic, Mohamad H. Danesh, Florence Clerc, Hsiu-Chin Lin, Jonathan Tremblay*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Regularised reinforcement learning (RL) has provable robustness guarantees that should ease the transfer of policies from simulation to robots, known as sim-to-real. Yet, weakly regularised algorithms like Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO) remain the de facto choice in sim-to-real pipelines, while the practical benefits of regularisation for transfer remain poorly characterised. In this work, we study the sim-to-real performance of commonly used RL algorithms, with the aim of characterising the effects of regularisation on robotic performance. To that end, we derive Soft MellowMax Actor-Critic (SMM-AC), a novel regularised RL algorithm based on the soft mellowmax value function, which carries strong robustness guarantees. On MuJoCo continuous control benchmarks, SMM-AC is competitive with baselines like Soft Actor-Critic (SAC). On real robots, SMM-AC achieves higher return, while exhibiting greater robustness to perturbations in observations, the environment, and dynamics than baselines. Together, these results demonstrate that regularised RL improves real-world policy deployment through better robustness to the sim-to-real gap, and changing real-world conditions.


### [98] [Sustainability of TSFM Deployment and Pretraining: From Carbon Footprint Analysis to Mitigation via Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32859)
*Cécile Luc, Nathanaël Lemonnier, Ievgen Redko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) offer strong zero-shot forecasting capabilities, but their high pretraining cost raises a practical sustainability question: does this upfront investment pay off over a full deployment lifecycle, compared to supervised standalone models that must be periodically retrained? We model the cumulative carbon cost of different approaches across a realistic industrial scenario: Chronos-2 amortizes its pretraining cost within months for large or frequently queried deployments, but a classical model remains cheaper indefinitely for small or rarely queried ones. For cases where reducing the TSFM's own cost matters, we show that removing a single encoder block (7.9% of parameters) via a gradient-free pruning criterion yields a consistent 6 to 9% reduction in FLOPs, CO2 emissions, and latency, at essentially no cost to zero-shot forecasting performance across a 25-task benchmark.


### [99] [PoKER: A Probability of Kill Estimation Rate Model for Air-to-Air Missiles Using Machine Learning on Stochastic Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32864)
*Joao P. A. Dantas, Andre N. Costa, Diego Geraldo, Marcos R. O. A. Maximo, Takashi Yoneyama*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This work introduces PoKER, a novel probabilistic model engineered to optimize missile launch effectiveness in air-to-air scenarios, specifically within Beyond Visual Range (BVR) air combat. Unlike conventional Weapon Engagement Zone (WEZ) models that delineate zones based on static distances such as maximum, minimum, and no-escape ranges, PoKER applies machine learning to predict kill probabilities more accurately by integrating the stochastic behaviors of targets and missile miss distances. This model dynamically evaluates target behavior, greatly expanding the predictive capabilities of engagement analysis. By factoring in elements such as warhead lethality, target and shooter orientations, and the specific conditions of engagement, PoKER provides important insights into engagement dynamics and quantifies success probabilities. Consequently, it can potentially be an important tool for BVR air combat pilots, improving operational decision-making within this specialized combat domain.


### [100] [Interpretable sparse fine-tuning with TopKLoRA enables faithful circuit discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32866)
*Marek Masiak, Andrzej Szablewski, Nicola Cancedda, Constantin Venhoff*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Fine-tuning on datasets of unknown origin can create a backdoor in the trained model when the data includes poisoned examples. Difficult to localise and remove, backdoors can persist through safety training. While parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods capture the full parameter changes introduced during training, they are not easily interpretable. We present the full method of TopKLoRA, extending the preliminary proposal of Masiak et al. (2025), an interpretable-by-design LoRA variant in which only the $k$ largest adapter latents are active at each token. Unlike SAEs, TopKLoRA is trained purely on downstream loss, where optimisation pressure incentivises learning functionally causal latents.
