In a direct adaptation, fixed reasoning intervals can trigger updates when nothing relevant has changed or postpone useful updates until the next scheduled step. Sharing one generation path also couples two distinct requirements: preserving evidence for future decisions and producing only the response requested by the user. Moreover, responses generated after reasoning must wait for it to finish, even when sufficient evidence is already available. To address these challenges, we introduce SyncPro, a synchronous dual-stream framework with specialized generation paths for evidence tracking and user-facing responses. Its Inner and Response branches share causal history and activate independently, allowing relevant evidence changes to trigger internal updates and responses to proceed concurrently with ongoing reasoning. We construct 189K dual-stream examples and train SyncPro through supervised fine-tuning followed by trajectory-level reinforcement learning. Experiments across three proactive benchmarks show improved response timing and more accurate answers to user requests.


### [289] [Depth-Dependent Safety Geometry in Vision-Language Models: Layer-Wise Localization and Bidirectional Refusal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32182)
*Muhammad Zeeshan Karamat, Syed Talal Hassan, Christiana Chamon Garcia*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are widely deployed, yet the internal mechanisms behind their safety behavior remain poorly understood. We present a lightweight inference-time framework for localizing and manipulating safety-sensitive representations in frozen VLMs, without retraining or modifying parameters. On Qwen3-VL-4B, layer-wise activation divergence analysis reveals non-uniform safety geometry across depth. Causal interventions show that early vision layers support strong bidirectional refusal control through activation steering along a learned safety direction, with unsafe rejection spanning 6%-96%, whereas later layers respond more to activation magnitude scaling and exhibit a substantial safety-selectivity trade-off. Deeper layers remain well separated along the safety direction yet are largely insensitive to steering, distinguishing representational separability from causal controllability. InternVL2.5-4B shows qualitatively similar depth-dependent activation geometry despite differences in layer location and magnitude. Our results expose a white-box, representation-level refusal-bypass attack surface in VLM vision encoders and motivate inference-time stabilization of safety-sensitive representations.


### [290] [Neural Disjunctive Normal Forms for Learning Exact Rank Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32203)
*Diego Marcondes, Wonder A. L. Alves, Ronaldo F. Hashimoto*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Gradient-based learning of morphological operators is hindered by locally sparse derivatives with respect to the structuring elements. We develop a method for learning rank filters that retains exact operator evaluation while distributing the derivatives across the window. Using threshold decomposition, we construct a neural disjunctive normal form representation and establish a sufficient condition for exactness with integer-valued images and structuring elements. We then derive a smooth surrogate from the unique root of a smooth threshold equation. Implicit differentiation gives closed-form derivatives with normalised local weights, whose distribution is controlled by a smoothing parameter. These derivatives are used in a straight-through procedure: the forward pass evaluates the exact rank filter, while the backward pass uses the surrogate. For fixed ranks, the procedure learns real-valued structuring elements of any rank filter, including the median, and extends to compositions, infima, and suprema of rank filters. Experiments on image denoising using MNIST and Fashion-MNIST and retinal vessel segmentation show that, starting from a generic random initialisation, the proposed approach consistently improves performance over training with exact subgradients in every setting. In image denoising, our best network also outperforms smooth morphological networks in all settings. On vessel segmentation, the learned erosions recover structuring elements that fit the vessels, as theoretically expected, whereas most of those trained with exact subgradients remain close to their initialisation.


### [291] [TileShare: Training-Free Acceleration of Overlapping Tiled ViT Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32233)
*Xiangzhong Ye, Zhongshen Li, Shen You, Ziyang Meng, Liang Xu, Junkai Ji, Qiuzhen Lin, Xiangtao Li, Ka-Chun Wong*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Fixed-window Vision Transformer (ViT) decoders run at deployment resolution as overlapping tiles: every token in an overlap band is processed by several full decoder instances. In the 2.4B-parameter MiniMax-H3 video decoder, about half of all per-token computation is duplicated. Removing the tiles fails because attention, global tokens and coordinates are bound to them. TileShare keeps each released tile's attention and computes every other operation once per token. The released computation changes at one kind of step only: an overlap token's per-tile results are blended into one shared state with the released weights. Interventions show that this blend costs quality where attention reads the shared states, mainly in the first few layers, which TileShare therefore keeps per tile. Without training, keeping six layers per tile decodes 1.38-1.51x faster than batched and compiled tiling on two GPU types (up to 1.96x with FP8) and is equivalent to the released decoder in PSNR, LPIPS and DISTS on DAVIS videos. On unseen UVG videos with the setting frozen beforehand, it matches PSNR and DISTS and improves LPIPS. TileShare carries over to two further decoders and is again confirmed on unseen data. It is equivalent in PSNR and DISTS on MAGI-1. On the ViTok-v2 image autoencoder at 2K-4K, it matches overlapping tiling in PSNR, improves LPIPS and DISTS and decodes 1.5-1.8x faster. How many layers stay per tile is measured once per model (for decoders, without labels); we report it for 14 decoders and encoders. Optionally, two GPU-hours of self-distillation let MiniMax-H3 share every layer without a loss in PSNR, and a coordinate field trained while the network stays frozen brings fully shared CLIP-based open-vocabulary segmentation to parity with sliding windows on seven benchmarks.


### [292] [RootDown: Deploying and Benchmarking Models for Beet-Root Detection on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32275)
*Gerardus Croonen, Andreas Trondl, Julia Simon, Daniel Steininger*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Quality inspection of sugar beets after harvest and storage is critical to preserve sugar content and ensure optimal processing efficiency. The transfer of harvested beets to field clamps or trucks provides an opportunity to visually inspect individual beets automatically on loader conveyors. However, this is a challenging task as the large number of beets causes partial overlap and occlusion, complicating both annotation and detection. In this work, we propose an end-to-end image-based pipeline for detecting partially occluded sugar beets in loading scenarios as a first step toward automated quality inspection. We introduce a dedicated benchmark and present an automatic annotation strategy that leverages the SAM 3 foundation model to generate bounding boxes. We compare models trained on manually annotated and automatically generated labels. Additionally, we investigate deployment considerations by benchmarking multiple inference configurations, including execution on an edge device, under realistic operational constraints regarding precision, inference time and power consumption. Our results show that, despite substantial occlusion and inherent annotation ambiguity, foundation-model-based labels can substitute for a substantial share of manual annotation, trading labeling effort for data volume at a modest accuracy cost. Furthermore, we demonstrate that a Raspberry Pi 5 equipped with a Hailo accelerator enables high-performance, low-power inference, making it a practical solution for mobile outdoor deployment.


### [293] [ReLEAP: Upcycling Vision Datasets through Cross-Task Unification and Semi-Supervised Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32289)
*Julia Simon, Daniel Steininger, Andreas Trondl*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Developing robust vision models requires large amounts of annotated data, especially for tasks involving fine-grained class distinctions under variable environmental conditions. Even in domains with numerous existing datasets, differences in annotation formats, label definitions and targeted learning tasks hinder data reuse and integration. To address this challenge, we introduce ReLEAP, a scalable Python framework for unifying heterogeneous image datasets through data-source abstraction, systematic reuse of annotations across learning tasks, and mapping of labels to a shared hierarchical taxonomy. We demonstrate its applicability in precision agriculture by integrating fifteen diverse datasets covering a broad range of plant species and annotation types. Beyond unification, ReLEAP enables systematic recycling of existing data and provides a robust foundation for learning-based annotation upcycling. Leveraging this foundation, we conduct extensive cross-dataset and fine-tuning experiments to investigate how domain-specific models trained on unified data and foundation models can be used to automatically generate higher-level annotations under unsupervised, weakly supervised and semi-supervised settings, and how dataset characteristics influence their effectiveness. Our results show that unified data access and annotation upcycling substantially expand the pool of reusable training data and annotations while improving data transparency, enabling more sustainable and resource-efficient use of existing datasets. We release the framework and trained models for academic use to support accessible and collaborative research, particularly in data-scarce and heterogeneous domains.


### [294] [GeoLEON-CLIP: Graph-Anchored Multimodal Contrastive Learning for Geographic Region Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32311)
*Radoslaw Malek, Szymon Soltysiak, Aleksandra Kawala-Sterniuk, Piotr Szymanski*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Geographic regions are multimodal: satellite imagery captures visual morphology, text conveys human-interpretable semantics, and map structures describe urban context. Existing geospatial contrastive methods typically align image-text or image-location pairs, underusing structured map data. We introduce GeoLEON-CLIP, a graph-anchored contrastive framework that aligns three views of each H3-indexed region: an OpenStreetMap-derived graph embedding, a satellite image crop, and a textual description. Frozen pretrained LEON graph embeddings provide a semantic anchor between vision and language. Frozen graph, image, and text encoders are connected by trainable residual projection heads and trained with a symmetric tri-modal contrastive objective. Experiments show strong graph-image and graph-text alignment; graph-image retrieval achieves the best cross-modal performance in a mixed-resolution pool. To evaluate the anchoring hypothesis, we propose chained cross-modal recall $\mathrm{R}@A@B$, which routes retrieval through the graph modality and compares it with direct retrieval under a matched candidate budget. At $\mathrm{R}@1@1$, image$\rightarrow$graph$\rightarrow$text and text$\rightarrow$graph$\rightarrow$image outperform direct image-text retrieval by 4.8 and 4.4 points, respectively, despite using a single intermediate cell. Downstream probes show task-dependent gains, with the largest improvements on Airbnb and Beijing housing. These results indicate that OpenStreetMap graph structure can anchor multimodal representations combining maps, imagery, and language.


### [295] [PROVE: Probabilistic Visual Grounding via Patch-Level Evidence for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32440)
*Mayank Nautiyal, Tianru Zhang, Li Ju, Ekta Vats, Prashant Singh*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Visual grounding with vision-language models such as CLIP typically produces a single deterministic relevance map for an image-query pair, without indicating whether the localisation is reliable or whether the referent is present at all. We introduce \textbf{PROVE} (\textbf{Pro}babilistic \textbf{V}isual Grounding via Patch-Level \textbf{E}vidence), a post-hoc evidential adapter that maps frozen CLIP patch features to per-patch Beta distributions without modifying the backbone. PROVE casts grounding as per-patch membership: each patch accumulates positive and negative evidence for whether it belongs to the referent, so a query may match zero, one, or many patches. We train PROVE with an evidential objective on soft patch-coverage targets, and define a prior-pull objective that keeps absent-referent examples near an uninformative prior, so that vacuity serves as an absence signal without a dedicated presence classifier. During inference, a single forward pass yields, in closed form, a posterior-mean grounding map, a spatial vacuity map, and an image-level absence score. We evaluate PROVE on referring-expression benchmarks: it is competitive in localisation with state-of-the-art CLIP grounding methods, its uncertainty tracks localisation error and supports selective prediction under abstention, and its image-level score detects when the referent is absent altogether. Our evidential patch-membership modelling offers a simple, drop-in mechanism for probabilistic verification in frozen vision-language grounding systems.


### [296] [PathJev: Decision-Native Vision-Language Modelling for Histopathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32478)
*Cristian Tommasino, Antonio M. Rinaldi*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Vision-language models used for decisions in histopathology rarely return calibrated probabilities over the candidates a user cares about. Decision-native models write a question and a candidate set chosen at run time into the input and return a normalised distribution from a small decision head in one forward pass. We build PathJev-P, a decision-native model for histopathology: MedGemma-1.5-4B with LoRA adapters and a decision head, trained on 40,000 patch states from three public collections whose expert labels are turned into typed questions with organ-specific candidate sets and a "none of these" option. In a comparison over 3 seeds with an analysis plan fixed before testing, on test sets without documented overlap with the training data of the backbone, PathJev-P is more accurate than a generative model trained with the same backbone, data and selection rule, the zero-shot pathology model CONCH and a general medical decision model (balanced accuracy 0.541 against 0.492, 0.345 and 0.213); its advantage over the generative model comes from two organs absent from training. The calibration advantage depends on how the generative model's probabilities are read out. With the summed candidate log-likelihood used in previous work, PathJev-P has the lower calibration error (ECE 0.204 against 0.279); with a length-normalised read-out of the same generative model, examined post hoc, the two have similar mean calibration error (0.201) and the generative model is better calibrated on the unseen organs, where PathJev-P remains over-confident. A supervised linear probe on a pathology foundation model is better calibrated in every setting. In a qualitative whole-slide demonstration, PathJev-P labels unsupervised tissue clusters with abstention and separates carcinoma from other tissue while running the model on a fraction of the tiles.


### [297] [LoCA: Enabling Multi-Modality in Geospatial Location Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32894)
*Panagiotis Koromilas, Eleanna Vali, Diogenis Antonopoulos, Eleni Saka, Dimitris Christofis, Nikolaos Ioannis Bountos, Konstantin Klemmer, Ioannis Papoutsis, Yannis Panagakis, Mihalis A. Nicolaou*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Location encoders map geographic coordinates to high-dimensional descriptors through contrastive alignment with paired Earth Observation imagery. We prove that this construction is structurally limited: any Lipschitz location encoder produces an output of intrinsic dimension at most 2, regardless of architecture or bandwidth, which forces the contrastive loss to strictly exceed its asymptotic optimum. Empirically, joint training also collapses the encoder's embedding covariance to low rank. Although dimension-limited, current location encoders sidestep this spectral collapse by training against a frozen image encoder, which we reinterpret as a covariance regularizer that supplies a high-rank target for the location encoder to fold onto. However, extending this approach to multimodal inputs is challenging, as location encoders should simultaneously map a single location to multiple, potentially incompatible, descriptors. We identify two requirements a regularizer must satisfy for location encoding in multimodal regimes: co-alignment across modalities, since adapters alone cannot align independently pretrained encoders; and high effective rank, since the location encoder inherits its target's spectrum. We propose LoCA (Location Contrastive Alignment), a two-stage pipeline that satisfies both. Stage 1 jointly trains Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 encoders with contrastive learning, producing a co-aligned, high-rank multimodal target. Stage 2 freezes the image encoders and trains a location and a tabular metadata encoder as projection adapters with multi-view contrastive learning. Against five state-of-the-art encoders on 17 tasks ranging from land cover and wildfire forecasting to geophysical, socio-economic, biodiversity and climate applications, LoCA achieves the most task wins, is the only model to outperform every other on a majority of tasks, and wins 68.8% of all per-task comparisons.


### [298] [Flownomaly: Single-Step Conditional-Mean Recovery for Few-Shot Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32964)
*Bernardo Perrone Ribeiro, Erkut Akdag, Egor Bondarev*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Few-shot industrial anomaly detection requires modeling category-specific normality from only a handful of defect-free images. Common approaches tie inference to stored exemplars, impose rigid linear structure, or risk reproducing anomalous content. Flownomaly instead casts the problem as normal-counterpart recovery in frozen DINOv2 feature space. Given normal supports, a velocity field is learned through conditional flow matching over frozen patch tokens to transport Gaussian noise toward normal features. Along straight paths, a single recovery step using one evaluation of the optimal field yields the posterior mean of the clean normal token without trajectory integration. The operating time governs how strongly recovery modifies a query token. With no anomalous validation samples available for tuning, a normal-only Jacobian gain-to-noise criterion selects this time from the supports alone. Squared residuals between query tokens and their recovered counterparts yield anomaly signals for both dense localization and image-level detection. Across 1-, 2-, and 4-shot benchmarks on MVTec-AD and VisA, together with sample-shot evaluations on Real-IAD, Flownomaly achieves state-of-the-art performance against recent category-specific methods. In the 1-shot setting, image- and pixel-level AUROC reach 97.8% and 98.0% on MVTec-AD and 95.3% and 98.7% on VisA, with a 2.1-point image-level AUROC improvement over the strongest baseline on VisA. Code is available at this https URL .


<div id='cs.CL'></div>

# cs.CL [[Back]](#toc)

### [299] [Validating the Comparison, Not Just the Scorer: Why Overall Accuracy Can Mislead in Interactive LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31678)
*Ancil Crayton*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Many LLM studies compare how models respond to different follow-up prompts. For example, one prompt may neutrally ask a model to reconsider, while another insists that an invented film really exists. The difference is used to measure the effect of conversational pressure. An automatic scorer then labels whether each response makes an unsupported factual claim. Researchers commonly validate the scorer with one overall accuracy or $F_1$ score. However, high overall performance can hide prompt-specific mistakes: if the scorer makes more errors after one prompt, the measured difference can be too large, too small, or point in the wrong direction. An audit containing mostly difficult responses can also misrepresent performance in the full study. We derive how such errors alter a prompt comparison and examine the problem using six models, 180 invented-entity questions, and six prompt conditions. In a balanced audit of 288 responses, overall $F_1$ was $.916$. Yet, depending on the prompt, the scorer flagged 2.6%-33.3% of responses that the primary LLM rater judged not to contain an unsupported claim; these prompt-specific estimates were uncertain. In a second audit, the scorer and two separate LLM raters evaluated the same 300 model-question cases under each prompt. An apparent 9.3-point increase from asserting certainty rather than neutrally asking the model to reconsider fell to 2.3 points under both raters. The primary-rater interval included zero, so it did not establish an increase. Two other prompt comparisons kept their direction under both raters, while an attempted prompt-specific correction remained too uncertain for a firm conclusion. Researchers should therefore validate the exact difference they plan to report, not just the scorer as a whole: audit both prompts on matching cases and include scorer and rater uncertainty in the final result.


### [300] [Rethinking Structured Generation: Can Graph-Based Reasoning Resolve Ambiguity?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31681)
*Ratun Rahman, Atit Pokharel*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Structured generation is typically formulated as a deterministic mapping from input to a single structured output. This assumption is fundamentally misaligned with natural language, which often admits multiple structurally distinct yet semantically valid interpretations. As a result, existing methods rely on single-reference supervision that collapses ambiguity into one prediction, leading to unstable and inconsistent outputs under underspecified inputs. We reformulate structured generation as an \textit{ambiguity-aware inference problem}, where the objective is to reason over a set of plausible structured outputs rather than predict a single solution. We show that standard training inherently suppresses valid alternatives, and instead adopt a multi-valid learning framework that preserves ambiguity during optimization. To operationalize this formulation, we construct a candidate space of structured outputs, represent each candidate as a graph capturing entities and their relationships, and perform selection via graph-based reasoning over semantic alignment and relational consistency. This enables principled comparison across competing interpretations beyond token-level likelihoods. Experiments on structured generation benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements in accuracy, stability, and robustness under varying levels of ambiguity. These results highlight the importance of modeling ambiguity explicitly and suggest a shift from single-output prediction to set-level reasoning in structured generation.


### [301] [An Agentic Approach with Evidence-Grounded Verification for Multimodal Emotion Reasoning in Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31685)
*Elyas Rashno, Farhana Zulkernine*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: LLM-based multimodal emotion reasoning systems perform well on standard benchmarks, but improving them through instruction tuning can be computationally expensive. Agentic pipelines built from frozen models avoid this cost, but introduce a different challenge: when a prediction is wrong, the system must identify which agent or stage caused the error. We address this problem with an agentic framework for traceable multimodal emotion reasoning and prompt-space adaptation. Modality-specific perception agents analyze visual, acoustic, and lexical inputs and post evidence-cited observations to a shared board. An integration agent combines these observations with retrieved multimodal examples to produce an emotion description, label set, and supporting citations, while an MLLM critic can request one revision before the output is finalized. For incorrect predictions, a separate evidence-grounded verification stage applies deterministic checks to verify that cited evidence and references are valid, and an entailment check to determine whether the final claims are supported by that evidence. Detected failures are then grouped by the responsible component and failure type, converted into targeted prompt patches, and accepted only after validation on a held-out set. This adapts the agent prompts without updating model weights. On the MER2025 dataset, targeted prompt adaptation outperforms the frozen static pipeline on all four MLLMs, ranging from 2B to 38B parameters, and matches or outperforms undifferentiated reflection baselines on each of them: VideoLLaMA3-2B, Phi-3.5-vision-instruct, Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct, and InternVL2.5-38B. It improves F-score by 0.6--2.2 points depending on the backbone, including an increase from 52.3 to 54.5 on Qwen2.5-VL-7B.


### [302] [From Target Selection to Condition Reuse: Auditing Residual Interventions in Tool-Use Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31686)
*Tianda Sun, Dimitar Kazakov*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Activation interventions can make a language model produce a target answer, but can the model use the intended condition in other computations? We study this question in Qwen3.5-27B on tool-use tasks where time or permissions determine eligibility and selection. Across 32 fresh worlds, two case-specific residual-writing variants raise familiar target-selection correctness from 35/128 to 119/128 and from 33/128 to 114/128. Yet these gains disappear when familiar facts and identifiers are recombined within the same selection task, while selection of previously rewarded identifiers increases. The gains persist across output formats but yield little improvement in independent judgments about whether a candidate is eligible or should be selected. Internal edits can nevertheless change how a source fact affects a judgment: in a prespecified secondary comparison, an early-layer source patch changes 50/64 judgments when that source is used, with one adverse change among 64 cases where it is not. This selective effect does not ensure correct complete actions. Together, these results separate target-selection control, condition-sensitive judgments, and complete-action correctness. Evaluating how an edited state is used, rather than only its trained answer, exposes these differences.


### [303] [Distributional Alignment of LLMs and Its Generalization Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31687)
*Nelson Jo*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate and probe human behavior, e.g., for surveys. For these applications, a key property is that outputs satisfy distributional alignment: to match its output distribution with some reference (in this case, human) distribution. An obstacle in achieving distributional alignment is generalizing from small training data. In this work, we study (1) how to distributionally align LLMs; and (2) how aligned LLMs might generalize to unseen samples and unrelated topics and tasks. Using two large-scale benchmarks, we find that LLMs can indeed achieve distributional alignment and generalize to unseen questions of the same domain. Cross-topic generalization depends on the relationship between topics (e.g., questions about politics are related to trust in institutions). However, aligned LLMs can degrade in surprising ways (e.g., lose their creative ability), suggesting that its generalization properties are inherently unpredictable and unstable.


### [304] [What the Query Remembers: Query Abstraction Governs Lexical and Dense Retrieval of Classical Arabic Poetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31821)
*Omar Farouk Zouak, Selma Mani, Larbi Said Chikh, Raid Abderrezak Ouahioune*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: How well a poetry archive can be searched depends on what the user still remembers, not only on what the model understands. We show this for Classical Arabic poetry, benchmarking nine dense encoders and BM25 over two corpora, three indexing modes and two evaluation protocols. Which retriever wins is governed by how much of a poem's wording the query still carries. On tip-of-the-tongue queries BM25 leads every dense encoder, including an 8B state-of-the-art model (0.746 vs. 0.717 Recall@5); on abstract queries it falls to eighth out of ten (0.392 vs. 0.748). Editing the wording of the same queries shows that this ranking largely reflects query composition: adding one remembered half-verse raises BM25 by 38 points, rewriting the quoted words by hand leaves BM25 level with the 8B encoder, and BM25's sensitivity to remembered wording is significantly steeper than that of the strongest dense encoder. The same axis controls explanation augmentation, whose effect swings from +37 to -39 points. The mechanism splits by system type: lexical containment predicts BM25's behaviour, while embedding proximity predicts dense encoders. Finally, the field's two standard protocols, known-item retrieval and thematic relevance, dissociate on the same systems, a result reproduced by two blind human annotators and by two open-weights judges, one of them under a rubric with no literal-match criterion. A benchmark that reports one protocol, or one query register, can give a materially wrong picture. Code and data are available at this https URL .


### [305] [Machine-Readable Operator Manuals over the ISO 11783 Network for Offline, Multilingual LLM Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31863)
*Timo Oksanen, Julius Gun*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: If agricultural machinery is to support intelligent, offline assistance, then the system must access technical documentation dynamically. In this paper, the Semantic Synchronization of operator manuals over ISO 11783-based CAN buses is analyzed, defined as the process that brings the local knowledge base of a tractor-side assistant into agreement with the operator manual of the connected implement. Data transfer performance was benchmarked using Markdown, JSON, and XML; various open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs) were also evaluated in cross-lingual query tasks. The automatic evaluation of the answers was validated against four human raters. A complete manual was transferred over a physical ISO 11783 network in 31 to 89 s, depending on format and transport settings, which is comparable to the object pool upload that operators already wait for at the first coupling of an implement. Markdown produced the smallest files, while the question-answering performance was comparable across the three formats for most models. With retrieval-augmented generation, locally run models answered most questions on the English manual correctly, with no advantage of the largest model over 7B and 8B models, but performed worse on the five other language editions. Keyword retrieval failed across languages, whereas semantic retrieval provided the cross-lingual capability. The exchange requires only a new parameter group that uses the existing request and transport mechanisms of ISO 11783, which makes it a candidate for standardization. The study is a proof of concept with inference on PC-grade hardware; deployment on embedded agricultural hardware remains future work.


### [306] [Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31945)
*Aleph Alpha, Moritz Althaus, Artur Baranowski, Michael Barlow, Fabien C. Y. Benureau, Felix Berkenkamp, Amrita Bhattacherjee, Lukas Blübaum, Duncan Blythe, Augustijn de Boer, Bastian Boll, Thomas F. Burns, Björn Deiseroth, Niko Dürr, Béni Balázs Egressy, Niklas Finken, Niklas Freymuth, Fabian Gebhart, Max Hammer, Bastian Harren, Andreas Hartel, Johann Higl, Steffen Hirschmann, Pablo Iyu Guerrero, Yasser Jadidi, Andreas Jagusch, Mitja Kleider, Markus Klein, Jonas Knupp, Murali Kondragunta, Garwin Lechner, Dominik Lê-Kellner, Jurek Leonhardt, Jiaze Li, Sohir Maskey, Omar Mehio, Pit Neitemeier, Letitia Parcalabescu, Alejandro Petry Pacheco, Pranav Ragupathy, Martin Reinhardt, Peter Rodenkirch Nfissi, Dylan Rodriquez, Leonard Salewski, Raziye Sari, Jordan Sassoon, Ivo Schaper, Ilhan Scheer, Frank Schneider, Philipp Scholl, Lukas Schott, Eric Schreiber, Pablo Schumacher, Alessio Serra, Philipp Siedler, Prabhu Teja Sivaprasad, Mohamed Soliman, Till Speicher, Volker Stampa, Simon Thel, Serge Thilges, Irina Vidal Migallón, Denis Waßmann, Samuel Weinbach, Sascha Wirges, Michael Wüthrich, Alexei Zacharov*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: This report introduces Kolibri, an English-German mixture-of-experts transformer with 78.1B total parameters and 3.46B active parameters per token, released as open weights under the Apache 2.0 licence. We built Kolibri for sovereign and specialised deployment, with a particular focus on German and on regulated domains such as public administration, industry, and aerospace. To support these settings, we control the full model-development process, including data, architecture, training infrastructure, post-training, and evaluation. We train Kolibri on 24T tokens across pre-training, mid-training, and long-context extension, with German accounting for more than 20% of the data mix by bytes. To reach this share, we complement German datasets with more than 2T German tokens that we curate from the web with our own pipeline and generate synthetically. Post-training combines supervised fine-tuning on curated German and English data with reinforcement learning on more than 1.2M internally curated tasks, which cover reasoning, tool use, instruction following, code, and retrieval. The model activates 4.4% of its parameters per token, and among the open models evaluated in this report, it lies on the Pareto frontier of quality and serving cost in English and German. Kolibri builds on Kolibri Origin, an earlier iteration that validated our full pipeline at scale. Both models come from our Model Factory, which connects data, experimentation, training, evaluation, and deployment through version-controlled workflows.


### [307] [Tampered ML: Evaluating LLM Judgment of Machine Learning Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32114)
*Soroush Ebadian, Parand A. Alamdari, Joe Benton, Hailey Joren*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: One approach to accelerating progress on AI alignment is to automate parts of alignment research itself. Doing so requires systems that can perform reliably on hard-to-supervise research tasks, including identifying flaws in research proposals and evaluating whether a proposed experiment supports its intended claim. To evaluate this capability, we introduce Tampered ML, a benchmark of 1,858 item-pairs, each consisting of an experiment proposal reconstructed from a published machine learning paper and a minimally edited counterfactual that introduces a targeted methodological flaw. Models must identify the more methodologically sound proposal in both presentation orders. A human validation study on a stratified sample estimates that 86% of items are valid (95% CI: 81-91%). Frontier models perform above chance but remain far from reliable (with Opus 5 achieving 77.9% accuracy). They are highly sensitive to the order of presentation and favor flawed designs that appear more specific, transparent, or conventional.


### [308] [The LLENS Platform for On-Premises Large Language Model Agents in a Japanese University Hospital: Development and Evaluation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32151)
*Ken Enda, Jien Saito, Masami Mukai, Feng Han, Emir Turkes, Tatsumi Kusakabe, Reona Umeki, Kazuhiro Sumiyoshi, Mitsuya Numata, Takumi Tanikawa, Akira Endoh, Kenji Hirata, Atsushi Yoshida, Kohsuke Kudo, Shinya Tanaka*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language model agents that use tools could take on part of the electronic health record and desk work that occupies physicians' time, but in many hospitals the medical information systems run inside closed networks, so the model and the agent harness must also sit inside. We designed, implemented, and deployed LLENS, a clinical support AI agent platform that runs inside the closed network of Hokkaido University Hospital on one host with 8 NVIDIA H200 GPUs, the open-weight model Kimi K2.6 (~1 trillion parameters), and a web application rebuilt from Open WebUI. The harness has 10 components, including page image reading, full-text attachment input, a path-addressed document tree without a vector index, a network-isolated sandbox, artifact return, self-checking of generated output, subagents, and saved procedures. Kimi K2.6 was chosen from 3 open-weight candidates and scored 480/500 on the 2026 Japanese Medical Licensing Examination; it generated 119 tokens/s for one user and served 16 concurrent requests at 908 tokens/s in total. On 180 questions over hospital documents (3 seeds each), mean accuracy was 10.7% with no documents, 81.3% with fixed retrieval (top 10 chunks of one lexical search), and 97.2% with interactive access, in which the model reads with document tools and the sandbox (15.9 percentage points above fixed retrieval, 95% CI 10.9-21.3); the difference lay in filtering/counting questions (16.7% vs 87.8%). Five scenarios with synthetic data ran on combinations of the components: needlestick injury response, batch adverse event grading, scanned referral letter transcription, falls data aggregation and plotting, and investigation of 3 topics by subagents. The platform has run on the hospital network since September 14, 2026.


### [309] [SOPO: Socratic Guided Policy Optimization for Span-Level Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32212)
*Jiawei Dong, Zilong Bai, Pengtian zhu, Peng Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained widespread adoption in various natural language processing tasks, but suffer from hallucination issues where they generate unfaithful or inconsistent content. While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has shown promising applications in reasoning tasks, its application to span-level hallucination detection is challenged by localization ambiguity, reward sparsity on hard examples, and uncontrolled length inflation. To address these issues, we introduce Socratic Guided Policy Optimization (SOPO), a multi-turn RL framework for robust hallucination detection. The key innovation of SOPO is a Socratic feedback mechanism, where a teacher model guides the reasoning trajectories during rollout by providing indirect hints inferred from the ground truth. This teacher-guided approach enables the model to generate more effective reasoning trajectories for hard examples, thus alleviating reward sparsity. Furthermore, we incorporate Turn-based Advantage Scaling to penalize verbosity bias, encouraging the model to develop efficient and autonomous reasoning capabilities. Empirical results on RAGTruth demonstrate that SOPO (4B) achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing leading proprietary models (e.g., GPT-5) and standard RL baselines, while obtaining higher reported mean scores than GRPO on four out-of-domain benchmarks, with mixed evidence against CAPO. We release our training and evaluation code alongside the 1.7B and 4B SOPO models at this https URL .


### [310] ["Not" is Not Lost in Translation: A Causal Analysis of Negation Representations in Multilingual LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32281)
*Giang Son Nguyen, Wray Buntine, Dung D. Le, Wenya Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Negation occurs in every language, yet its surface realization differs drastically. Despite this, multilingual large language models (LLMs) translate negation robustly, raising the question of whether they share an underlying representation of negation across languages. We study this question in six open models across English, French, Vietnamese, and Chinese, using causal interventions on a synthetic contrastive dataset and a negation translation dataset derived from FLORES-200. Across models, we find strong evidence that negation representations are causally controllable and largely shared across languages. In particular, we show that a contrastive activation direction extracted from one language can induce or remove negation in the others, and directions extracted from different languages are substantially aligned. We further find that negation is represented in sparse features: a single sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature per model suffices to control polarity across languages, while responding selectively to negated inputs. Finally, both dense and sparse interventions generalize to four additional languages and FLORES-derived sentences. We release data and code to support future work: this https URL .


### [311] [Decoupling Patient Personas and Clinical Workflows: A Dual-Agent Approach to Realistic Medical Dialogue Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32443)
*Qiyuan Li, Xiaomei Guo, Chao Gao, Haijiang Liu, Deyu Chen, Caicai Guo, Jinguang Gu*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Pre-consultation medical dialogues are essential for collecting comprehensive patient information, but are costly to obtain at scale due to privacy and ethical constraints. Existing LLM-based synthesis methods often rely on prompt engineering or rigid rules, limiting their ability to balance clinical relevance with realistic patient diversity. We propose CoRPS-Dial, a controllable dual-agent framework for pre-consultation medical dialogue synthesis that integrates patient personas with guideline-aligned clinical workflow rules. CoRPS-Dial generates dialogues through interactions between a persona-driven patient agent and a process-guided doctor agent, followed by post-hoc quality control for role consistency, workflow completeness, and textual consistency. Experiments on coronary heart disease demonstrate that CoRPS-Dial improves alignment between synthetic and real dialogues in information coverage, clinical compliance, and dialogue naturalness. Downstream fine-tuning experiments further confirm the utility of the generated synthetic data for medical dialogue tasks. These results highlight the effectiveness of CoRPS-Dial for clinically grounded and controllable medical dialogue synthesis.


### [312] [Analysing Austrian Parliamentary Speeches by Predicting Party Affiliations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32515)
*Sarah Sulollari, Vanja Karan, Benjamin Roth*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: This paper examines speech characteristics of the Austrian National Council members (CMs). We introduce analysis methodology based on a fine-tuned BERT-Model which assigns to every speech a probability distribution over Austrian parties. These are then aggregated at the speaker and party level. We perform an extensive evaluation to ensure the quality of the generated distributions prior to further analysis. Instead of considering mis-assignments of predicted parties as a performance issue, we leverage them to derive insights about the CMs and political developments. Our findings show that parties that govern together become more similar in their speech characteristics, while, analogously, parties that are in opposition move further away from governing parties. Moreover, CMs who switch party usually adapt their speech characteristics only after the switch occurs. Finally, we publicly release a data set containing party affiliation predictions for the speeches of the Austrian National Council from 1996 to 2018.


### [313] [Ontology-Guided Knowledge Graph Construction from Structured Metadata and Climate Science Publications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32725)
*Aayush Acharya, Qi Zhang, Eduard Dragut, Longin Jan Latecki*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: The rapid expansion of scientific literature far outpaces the speed of manual curation, creating a fundamental bottleneck in extracting structured knowledge from scientific texts. To address this, we introduce an agentic, ontology-guided framework for knowledge graph (KG) construction. Our framework utilizes specialized agents for document understanding, information extraction, ontology conformance, and entity linking. First, an LLM extracts factual knowledge in the form of triples with explicit textual evidence from research papers. Then the triplets undergo a multistage verification process to ground the triplets in the ontology. By enforcing schema constraints and requiring evidence in the form of text fragments, we ensure the data is both semantically consistent and traceable. In an agreement study over 1,000 extracted triplets, Gemini-2.5-flash achieves Cohen's $\kappa$ scores of 0.640--0.700 against three human annotators, matching the human--human agreement range of 0.616--0.689. We applied this framework to 2,000 scientific publications and construct a novel, ontology-grounded KG for climate science with 133,990 nodes and 755,308 relations; of the 23,327 triplets that the publications contribute after validation, 98.96\% are judged correct. The pipeline code and KG is publicly available this https URL .


### [314] [On-Policy Self-Training: Data-Free Self-Improvement in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32770)
*Leon Sick, Lukas Hoyer, Yannick Strümpler, Christian Reisswig, Timo Ropinski, Federico Tombari*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation enables post-training foundation models without relying on stronger external teachers. However, existing approaches rely on ground-truth question-answer pairs, human-curated problem sets, or other supervised signals. In this work, we reformulate on-policy self-distillation as a self-training framework, driven by self-generated question-answer pairs and privileged reasoning demonstrations. We push supervision to the limit: guided solely by coarse, high-level topic descriptions, the model synthesizes its own curriculum of queries and privileged reasoning traces without access to any human-curated prompts or answers. We find that self-distillation on fully synthetic privileged information enables a model to improve across multiple domains while retaining its capabilities on domains it is not self-trained on. We demonstrate the generality of this framework across text and multimodal domains, achieving consistent self-improvement on standard language benchmarks and Visual Question Answering. Our findings demonstrate that foundation models possess rich latent capabilities that can be amplified without external task supervision or curated problem distributions.


### [315] [Every Edge Needs a Quote: Building a Quote-Grounded Knowledge Graph of Healthcare Software Adoption with Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32781)
*Juan Felipe Duran, Arthur Tobler, Martin Vallières*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Hospitals regularly buy software that clinicians and patients depend on. Because these purchases are costly, hard to reverse and can affect patient care, buyers look for evidence of which peer institutions already use a product and which systems it connects to. This evidence is public but scattered across vendor websites, press releases and third-party pages. Language-model agents can now collect it at low cost, but the correctness of the extracted claims is difficult to establish, and a wrongly listed customer can mislead buyers and misrepresent an organisation. We present a pipeline that builds a knowledge graph of customer and integration relations from the open web under one rule: every edge must be supported by a verbatim quote from its source page. Pages come from a deterministic crawl of each vendor's site and a budgeted ReAct agent that searches the rest of the web. A permissive extractor proposes relations with quotes, and a layered grounding stage decides which to keep, using a referent step that reads the whole page when a quote names only one side, deterministic checks, and a panel of five model calls voting on nine criteria. On 61 vendors, the pipeline reaches 0.94 precision and 0.71 recall against a page-level key covering the 6,428 candidate relations proposed by its extractor, whose verdicts were drafted by one model and re-checked with a second. Against the golden reference, the marketplace's own curated list, it scores lower, but 89% of the relations it keeps that the reference lacks are stated on a page. A confidence score built from the panel's votes keeps more correct relations than the fixed rule at the same precision, and reaches 0.90 precision at 0.90 recall. We also discuss how narrow, bounded model calls, string-matched evidence and voting address long-context degradation and run-to-run variance.


### [316] [Yalda-Embedding: Open Development of a Competitive Persian Retrieval Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32933)
*Yalda Taheri, Mohammad Hassan Heydari, Armon Rasooli*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Despite advances in multilingual text representations, effective dense retrieval for Persian remains constrained by the limited availability of language-specific training resources and compact embedding models. We present Yalda Embedding, a 595.8-million-parameter Persian retrieval model adapted from Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B using more than one million query-document pairs. On eight Persian retrieval tasks from FaMTEB, the model achieves the highest macro-average among the evaluated 0.6B models, scoring 0.5488 and outperforming Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B and F2LLM-v2-0.6B on every task. It also approaches Qwen3-Embedding-4B, winning on four tasks, tying on one, and trailing by 0.0075 in macro-average. An incremental ablation study examines contrastive batch size, optimization stabilization, and relational knowledge distillation from F2LLM-v2-8B. Under the one-epoch training setup, contrastive fine-tuning alone yields a modest mean improvement over initialization and regressions on two tasks. The final distilled configuration improves all eight tasks over the non-distilled model, raising the mean from 0.4813 to 0.5488 without increasing inference-time model size. These gains include hard-negative evaluation tasks despite training without explicitly mined or supplied hard negatives. We release model weights, training and evaluation code, the pairwise dataset, and its teacher-embedded counterpart, enabling inspection of data composition and training procedures, independent evaluation, and reuse without repeating expensive teacher inference.


### [317] [Multi modal perceived conversation success prediction for spontaneous autistic and non-autistic dyadic interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31682)
*Thanushi Withanage, Desi Jones, Noah Sasson, Elizabeth Redcay, Carol Espy-Wilson*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Conversation success is important for communication, social bonds, and well-being and is often reduced in autistic-neurotypical conversations. Previous studies have predicted perceived conversation success using acoustic and lexical features in pediatric autism-enriched samples, or using nonverbal behavior in non-autistic group interactions. Other work has characterized prosodic, facial, motor, and turn-taking synchrony in autistic interactions, but has primarily targeted diagnostic classification or group differences. The present study instead predicts participant perceived conversation success from complementary articulatory, facial, acoustic-prosodic, and turn-taking measures across three spontaneous dyadic-interaction datasets including autistic and non autistic individuals. By leveraging multimodal features, we demonstrate that conversational success is inherently multidimensional and requires a multi-faceted analytical framework. Combining these complementary modalities offers the potential to improve prediction of perceived conversation success by capturing information unavailable from any single modality.


### [318] [How Much Do You Trust A Dog? Quantifying Hint-Following in Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32055)
*Anxin Guo, Chirag Pabbaraju, Sara Ahmadian, Kamesh Munagala, Aravindan Vijayaraghavan*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, altering their answers to align with user suggestions even on objective factual and reasoning tasks. Yet the quantitative form of this influence--- and whether it is calibrated to source reliability--- remains poorly understood. In this work, we investigate this behavior through hint-induced answer shifts on MMLU questions, evaluating prompts of the form ``[Source] thinks the answer is (X)''. We systematically vary the source to which the hint is attributed (a professor, a friend, or a dog) across three open-source model families, Qwen, Gemma, and SmolLM. We show that the change induced by the hint follows a predictable structure well-characterized by an exponential tilt: the hint induces an approximately constant mean shift in the hinted answer's log-odds, with question--target susceptibility largely shared across sources. To explore how this pattern can arise, we introduce a formal decomposition of the hint response and illustrate in controlled synthetic experiments how KL-regularized reward-based training reshapes both hinted and unhinted answer distributions. Finally, we evaluate whether this shift is calibrated to source reliability, finding that the log-odds shift persists even when the hint-giver is declared inaccurate or accompanied by explicit warnings not to trust the source, highlighting the vulnerability of LLMs to adversarial steering.


### [319] [Semantically Rich, Metrically Affect-Blind: A Geometric Blind Spot in Modern Text Embedders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32122)
*Mahdi Naser Moghadasi*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: We report a systematic dissociation in modern text embedders: sentiment is almost perfectly decodable from their embeddings, yet almost entirely absent from their metric. Across eleven frozen encoders spanning 2021 to 2026, including 2026-generation models and the 4B-parameter LLM embedder Qwen3-Embedding-4B, a linear probe recovers sentence polarity at up to 91.3% accuracy while cosine similarity separates polarity by a margin of only 0.003-0.064. Qwen3-Embedding-4B, the strongest probe in the panel, reaches 91.3% probe accuracy but only 64.3 retrieval MAP. Because retrieval, clustering, deduplication, and retrieval-augmented generation operate on the metric rather than on a trained probe, these systems are effectively blind to information their encoder demonstrably contains. A task-conditioning control suggests this reflects the pretraining objective rather than a prompting artifact: conditioning Jina-v5 on its classification task rather than its retrieval task recovers only about half of the MAP gain that injection achieves on the same weights (63.2 to 73.8, versus 85.1), and leaves the alignment gap essentially unchanged. Sentiment-Injected Sentence Embeddings (SISE) train a small (0.13M-0.69M-parameter) supervised-contrastive head on a frozen encoder in seconds on a CPU, using the labelled training set. The head improves every encoder released since 2022 on full-data probe accuracy (up to +5.8 points), retrieval MAP (up to +26.0) and polarity margin (7-61x). This is a specialization, not a free gain: the compact 128-d head loses 38-41 Spearman points on STS-B, while a fused variant that concatenates it with the base embedding loses 2.4-12.0. With the head, a 0.6B encoder at 128 dimensions matches a 4B encoder given the same head (91.8 vs. 90.3 MAP). We release a CPU-reproducible pipeline and a reusable diagnostic, the Attribute Alignment Gap.


### [320] [Do Halting Heads Really Save Compute in Looped Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32246)
*Tsung-Han Wu, Minsuk Heo, Kenny Tan*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Looped language models run each token through the same layers several times, and each run is one iteration. To save compute, an extra layer, the halting head, gives after each iteration the probability that the token stops there, so that easy tokens can stop early. Pretraining models from scratch, up to 1.3B parameters, we find two problems. First, heads trained on a mix of all iterations predict that tokens need under half the iterations, yet under the standard stopping rule tokens run almost every iteration, because this training never scores the single iteration where a token stops. Scoring every iteration instead fixes this, but in our runs it works only with a strong penalty favoring early stops. Second, savings are commonly reported as an average number of iterations per token, but when sequences are generated in batches that run until their slowest token stops, a model without a head is almost as good at that cost. The reported average can therefore overstate the saving. We recommend reporting the iterations tokens actually run, with the stopping threshold and batch size, and give a test, run before training a head, that bounds its gain.


### [321] [Acoustic and Lexical Signals for Native Language Identification of Language Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32382)
*Ahmet Yavuz Uluslu, Mengjie Qian, Kate Knill, Mark Gales*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Spoken native language (L1) identification for second language learners has traditionally relied on acoustic attributes of speech. Comparatively little attention has been paid to analysing speech transcripts for L1 traces. In this paper, we present a study of L1 identification using both speech and corresponding transcripts from a spoken learner English corpus. We compare a WavLM-based audio L1 classifier with a zero-shot GPT-4o classifier operating on transcripts and analyse their predictions across proficiency levels using a CEFR-balanced evaluation set. Our experiments show that the audio classifier outperforms the transcript-based approach overall when full transcripts are available, although their relative strengths vary with learner proficiency. Audio performs better at A1, whereas text performs better from A2 to C1 with full transcripts. Transcript-based accuracy generally increases with proficiency, while acoustic performance remains comparatively stable. Restricting transcript length substantially reduces text-based accuracy, whereas audio is less sensitive to input length and performs relatively well with limited input. These findings provide insights into L1 identification from acoustic and textual representations of learner speech and how performance varies with proficiency and the amount of available language material.


### [322] [Adaptive Frequency Threshold Byte Pair Encoding for Mixed Natural Language and Structured Symbolic Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32921)
*Pietro Di Nicco, Lucio La Cava, Andrea Tagarelli*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Modern language models rely on subwording tokenization to define the discrete units of input processing, typically using methods like Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) that treat whitespace as a fixed semantic boundary. This design is inherently limited, as many meaningful concepts---including multiword expressions and the structured environments of formal languages like LaTeX---frequently span across whitespaces. While recent superword tokenization methods attempt to address this by relaxing whitespace constraints, they often rely on greedy frequency-driven merging that fails to account for token length or the specific needs of underrepresented domain-specific languages. In this paper, we introduce Adaptive Frequency Threshold Byte Pair Encoding (AdaBPE), a new tokenization method that leverages an adaptive frequent-pattern mining approach. AdaBPE features a twofold dynamicity: it adapts minimum frequency thresholds based on both the length of a candidate token, to prevent favoring frequent short patterns, and its type, to distinguish between general and formal language patterns, using LaTeX as case in point. This allows the model to efficiently allocate vocabulary capacity to domain-specific notations, such as LaTeX, even when they represent a small fraction of the training data. We evaluate AdaBPE across five datasets spanning LaTeX and natural language, showing consistent gains in encoding efficiency and downstream language-model performance on domain-specific content without compromising general-language performance.


### [323] [Measure Before You Train: Typed Decision Models vs. a Classical Baseline vs. a Generative LLM for Routing a Personal Multilingual Inbox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32955)
*Federico Lucca*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Personal email agents decide, for every message, what it is, whether it is urgent, whether to draft a reply, create a task, or ignore it. Their error rate is rarely measured: the stored labels were written by the system itself. We present Foyer, a self-hosted personal inbox agent in daily use, and a measurement protocol (stratified sampling, frozen snapshots, thread-grouped splits, pre-registered hypotheses, human labels in the agent's review loop) that yields ground truth before any training. On 739 real Italian/English emails labelled by the owner (231 unbiased, 500 adversarially sampled), we compare nine systems under thread-grouped cross-validation: the production keyword rules, a TF-IDF + logistic-regression baseline, local typed decision models (Tev1, Nimble), a hosted decision model (Nimble v4-max), and hosted generative LLMs (Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma 4 26B/31B). Four results. The production rules score 28% on message type against a 58% majority class: the field shown most prominently carried no information. With ~200 labels, logistic regression has the highest accuracy on every field (type 0.79, urgency 0.74, needs-reply 0.83, is-noise 0.90), though it never predicts the two rarest classes and is statistically tied with Gemma 4 on type; few-shot prompting does not close the gap. The sender's relationship (client, supplier, service) is recovered from other emails of the same contact with 98.6% accuracy where the contact has been seen, and is not inferable from a single message by any model: it belongs in the contact graph, not the prompt. Finally, accuracy is not the product metric: composed into actions, the most accurate system most often ignores emails needing action (18% vs 4% for the best LLM); a cost-chosen threshold on its noise probability halves that rate and makes it the cheapest system.


<div id='cs.DS'></div>

# cs.DS [[Back]](#toc)

### [324] [Learning Positive Linear Combinations of ReLUs in Fully Polynomial Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32940)
*Ilias Diakonikolas, Jelena Diakonikolas, Puqian Wang, Nikos Zarifis*

Main category: cs.DS

Abstract: We study the fundamental problem of PAC learning positive linear combinations of $k$ arbitrary ReLU activations on $\mathbb{R}^d$ with respect to the Gaussian distribution, in $L_2$ loss. We give the first fully-polynomial time algorithm for this learning task. Specifically, our algorithm draws $\mathrm{poly}(d, k, 1/\epsilon)$ samples, runs in $\mathrm{poly}(d, k, 1/\epsilon)$ time, and outputs a hypothesis that is $\epsilon$-close to the target concept with high probability. All prior algorithms for this problem require super-polynomial time in $k$ or $1/\epsilon$, or leverage strong well-conditioned assumptions on the weight matrix. On a technical level, our approach develops a novel boosting procedure of a ``weak'' learner that recovers the hidden weight vectors to constant error, in a list-decodable manner. To develop this ``weak'' learning procedure, we leverage implicit tensor computation techniques to construct a low-degree filtering procedure based on Hermite tensors of the target function. This procedure amplifies separation among (clusters of) weight vectors to learn each of them to constant error. We then argue that the same procedure can be used for improving the estimation of hidden weight vectors, using gradient descent-like updates on the Euclidean sphere. A crucial ingredient of our algorithm is a pruning approach that ensures only relevant vectors that approximate the hidden weight vectors at target accuracy are maintained by the algorithm.


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [325] [A Model-Agnostic Correction Framework for Short-Term Building Load Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31959)
*Shiqiao Zhou, Huanbo Lyu, Holger Schöner, Zipeng Wu, Jessica Deuschel, Freya Wentzel, IAG Wilson, Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: Short-term building load forecasting remains challenging because load patterns can change rapidly with weather and operating conditions. Data-driven forecasting models may become less reliable when the current operating regime is rare or poorly represented during training, even when similar situations exist elsewhere in the historical record. Recent advances in retrieval-augmented time series forecasting offer a promising way to adapt predictions to changing conditions by directly reusing relevant historical cases at inference time. Motivated by this idea, we propose WeL-RAC, a model-agnostic Weather-Load Retrieval-Augmented Correction framework that explicitly retrieves such evidence at prediction time. WeL-RAC retrieves historical cases that are similar to the current condition based on both recent load states and horizon-aligned weather forecasts. Their observed future load trajectories are then aggregated and used to correct the initial prediction, without retraining or modifying the base forecaster. We evaluate WeL-RAC on 31 building cases across three datasets, eight forecasting backbones, and four horizons from 1 to 18 hours. The framework improves the corresponding base forecasters in 90 of 96 dataset-level comparisons. Beyond accuracy, success and failure case studies further illustrate the framework's interpretability by tracing how retrieved historical evidence improves or degrades forecasts. These findings support explicit historical retrieval as an effective and interpretable complement to the building load forecasting.


### [326] [Knowledge Distillation across Item and Code Spaces for Parallel Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32943)
*Yixuan Xu, Hailin Liu, Linxiang Peng, Can Yang*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: Generative recommendation with semantic IDs represents each item as a sequence of discrete codes derived from its semantic representation and predicts these codes to generate recommendations. Existing semantic-ID recommenders are typically trained with hard labels for the observed next item. Although such supervision identifies the target, it provides no explicit distributional information about alternative items conditioned on the same user history. Sequential recommenders can supply this item-level predictive knowledge, but transferring it to semantic-ID generation is challenging because the teacher and student operate in item and code spaces, respectively. We propose two complementary knowledge-distillation objectives for parallel semantic-ID recommenders, which predict all code positions simultaneously without autoregressive prefix conditioning. Item Distribution Matching (IDM) reconstructs a catalogue-level item distribution from the student's parallel code predictions and aligns it with the teacher's item distribution. Code Marginal Matching (CMM) projects the teacher's item probabilities onto each code position using fixed item-to-code assignments, then matches the resulting code marginals to the corresponding student heads. Both objectives transfer the teacher's soft distributional knowledge while preserving the student's parallel prediction structure. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that both methods consistently outperform existing state-of-the-art approaches. On average, IDM improves Recall@10 by 15.39%, while CMM improves NDCG@10 by 8.28%. These results demonstrate that distillation in either reconstructed item space or code-marginal space can substantially strengthen hard-label supervision for parallel generative recommendation.


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [327] [A Dynamic Inventory Rationing Model for Last-Mile Delivery of Medication in Liberia's Community Health Program](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32051)
*Baris Ata, Yufan Chen, Robert Montgomery*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: We study the dynamic allocation of scarce medications in Liberia's community health program. A central warehouse supplies a health facility and the Community Health Assistants (CHAs) it supervises. When a CHA runs out of medication, patients are referred to the facility, incurring travel costs if they complete the referral and remain untreated otherwise. Allocating inventory to CHAs allows more patients to receive treatment without traveling, while retaining inventory centrally preserves flexibility to respond to uncertain demand. The allocation problem must balance these considerations while accounting for fairness across locations. We formulate this problem as a finite-horizon stochastic control problem whose objective combines the costs of referrals and untreated patients with a demand-weighted fairness utility. Because standard dynamic programming methods are computationally intractable in this setting, we develop a continuous-time approximation that involves controlling the drift rate of a reflected Brownian motion. We then adapt the deep splitting method, using deep neural networks to approximate the value function and its gradient and compute a dynamic allocation policy. We calibrate the model using data on malaria cases among children in the Fangonda region of Lofa County, Liberia, and evaluate the proposed policy in the original discrete-time model. In the baseline setting, our policy reduces operating costs by about 6% relative to the strongest benchmark, the fairness-only policy, while retaining approximately 98% of its fairness utility. Additional experiments show that higher referral costs shift inventory toward CHAs and reduce local unmet demand, while greater demand variability increases our policy's performance over the fairness-only benchmark. Our model and computational approach provide a framework for studying similar last-mile allocation problems.


### [328] [On the Limits of Stochastic Push-Pull over Time-Varying Digraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32422)
*Feiming Wang, Yutong He, Sulaiman Alghunaim, Kun Yuan*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: Stochastic Push-Pull achieves linear speedup with respect to the network size $n$ on static strongly connected digraphs, but whether this speedup persists on time-varying networks remains unclear. In this work, we construct a quadratic decentralized problem on period-two networks showing that stochastic Push-Pull may fail to attain linear speedup over time-varying digraphs. For $p$-periodic networks, we identify an effective node count $n_{\mathrm{eff}}\le n$ that governs the leading stochastic term. Specifically, for smooth nonconvex objectives, we establish convergence upper bounds and matching lower bounds for stochastic Push-Pull with constant stepsizes, both exhibiting the leading-order dependence $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{eff}} T})$. When $p=1$, the identity $n_{\mathrm{eff}}=n$ recovers the usual linear speedup for static digraphs. However, periodic networks can have $n_{\mathrm{eff}}=o(n)$, demonstrating the loss of linear speedup under temporal variation. Numerical experiments illustrate effective-count scaling and the separation between static and periodic networks, consistent with our theoretical findings.


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [329] [Probing the limits of forward screening and inverse design of crystal materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32678)
*Zhilong Song, Zijun Pang, Lixue Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

Abstract: Crystal materials are designed either by screening known crystals for a target property or by generating new crystals with that property. Both routes are judged against public databases of known crystals, but these databases name the same crystal differently. Several materials announced as new by recent generative and autonomous discovery campaigns have since proved to be known phases or close variants of them. The limits of both routes remain unmeasured. Here we probe them by matching the crystals of 55 databases by structure into one self-extending database of 14.6 million crystals and 131.8 million property values, each traced to its source. Only 4.5% of its crystal-property pairs carry a value. To fill the empty pairs, we developed CGTNet, an equivariant graph transformer with state-of-the-art accuracy on several tasks of two public benchmarks. Trained on each task, it forms a library of property models whose predictions we release. The reach of forward screening has been underestimated. Known crystals hold tens of thousands of ultra-incompressible candidates without a recorded bulk modulus, and a post hoc audit of synthesis evidence finds among them ReN2, whose bulk modulus was measured at 428 GPa. The novelty claimed for inverse design has been overestimated. At an equal verification budget, a stiffness-conditioned generator returned more stiff hits than screening (100 versus 55), but all contained osmium and 82 repeated recorded compositions. Integrating and completing the databases of known crystals therefore moves the limits of both routes: it widens what screening can reach and sets the reference against which generated crystals are judged new.


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [330] [SILMARILS-MV: Information-Theoretically Secure Multi-Verifier Signatures with Dealer-Free Key Issuance and Linear Aggregation for Permissioned Blockchains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31814)
*Khadijeh Bagheri, Hassan Khodaiemehr, Chen Feng*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Permissioned blockchains authenticate transactions, votes and quorum certificates with computationally secure signatures, and post-quantum replacements inflate consensus traffic by orders of magnitude. Information-theoretic alternatives have required a trusted authority, quantum channels, per-signature interaction, or single-use keys with bounded transfer. We present SILMARILS-MV, a multi-verifier unconditionally secure signature in which a signature is the vector $\alpha = A\,e(m)$ and each verifier holds a secret functional $c_i = \Gamma^T \lambda(x_i)$ derived from virtual anchors $\Gamma$ that nobody learns. Against unbounded adversaries corrupting up to $\omega$ verifiers and any signers, and observing every decision, an honest verifier accepts a forged or invalid signature with probability at most $Q/q + 1/(q-1)$, a bound that is tight in its leading term. The scheme is strongly unforgeable, non-repudiable, transferable across unboundedly many hops, and linearly aggregatable. Keys are issued by the validators through perfectly secure MPC for $t < n/3$, without broadcast. To scale, we let the committee itself sign: in threshold notarization, finality certificates have constant size $32(\omega+1)$ bytes and per-validator issuance cost no longer depends on $n$. Arbiter-verified keys give clients 32-byte signatures, robust redistribution supports membership changes and share refresh, and verifiers can be enrolled retroactively. A certificate verifies in 2.4 microseconds. In an analytic normal-case model at 1000 validators, threshold notarization needs 71 KiB of authentication traffic per validator per block, 22x less than ML-DSA-44 for admitted verifiers. In a Rust HotStuff prototype with 100 validators, SILMARILS-MV commits 2.8x more transactions per second than ML-DSA-44 with 67x less cryptographic CPU, and Monte-Carlo attacks match the bounds.


### [331] [Agentic AI Defense: Decode-Time Provenance Binding for MCP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31974)
*Eric Yocam, Varghese Vaidyan, Paris Kalathas, Patrick Tague, Bruce DeBruhl, Yong Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Agentic AI systems using the Model Context Protocol (MCP) are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection through untrusted tool outputs. We demonstrate cross-modal trust laundering, in which instructions concealed in an email-signature image using least-significant-bit (LSB), discrete cosine transform (DCT), or metadata steganography bypass text scanners and enter a multi-agent workflow when decoded by a tool. To counter this threat, we propose decode-time provenance binding, which combines HMAC-authenticated trust labels with an MCP-maintained provenance ledger and context-taint tracking. The mechanism labels decoded content as untrusted and blocks critical tool calls influenced by that content, even when a compromised agent substitutes a valid trusted label. We evaluate the approach using a real MCP testbed with three steganographic carriers and 24 adaptive payloads. The undefended pipeline exhibits 100% attack success, while keyword and regex defenses each permit 62.5% of attacks. Our mechanism blocks all attacks (0% success), including adversarial label substitution across mediated agent boundaries. A four-component ablation validates the roles of decode marking, cryptographic authentication, lineage tracking, and sink enforcement. An audited trusted-promotion mechanism increases legitimate-task completion from 67% to 100% without compromising protection. The approach provides carrier-independent containment within fully mediated MCP execution environments. Code and data are publicly available.


### [332] [AgentTrace: Execution-Level Fingerprinting of Black-Box LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32092)
*Gejian Zhao, Hanzhou Wu, Xinpeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Verifying the provenance of autonomous LLM agents is a critical security challenge. Existing semantic attribution is easily obfuscated by system prompts, while active watermarking conflicts with schema-constrained tool generation. We propose AgentTrace, an active framework that identifies the foundation model behind a black-box agent via observable execution traces. AgentTrace injects structurally ambiguous probes to trigger tokenization-sensitive serialization failures at rigid tool interfaces. While these initial failures provide coarse-grained separation across model families, the temporal sequence of subsequent repair operations reveals fine-grained, model-specific recovery behaviors. By modeling these multi-step trajectories with lightweight Markov chains, AgentTrace achieves 96.0% attribution accuracy under adversarial identity spoofing. Crucially, sequential repair modeling successfully distinguishes the evaluated intra-family variants sharing identical tokenizers (e.g., Llama-3 8B/70B), substantially outperforming unordered behavioral statistics while remaining robust against prompt obfuscation and temperature scaling.


### [333] [Data-Efficient Backdoor Attacks on Code Models via Trigger-Neutralized Counterfactual Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32334)
*Yu-Yu Lee, Ming-Feng Tsai, Tomohiro Morikawa, Chun-I Fan*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Pre-trained code models are exposed to backdoor poisoning, in which crafted training samples make the model misbehave on a trigger. A capable attacker minimizes the number of such samples, since each adds cost and exposure and a lower poisoning rate leaves a smaller footprint for defenses.
