Which samples to poison therefore matters, but existing data-efficient selection methods target image classification, offer no scoring mechanism for autoregressive generation, and have not been studied on code. We propose Trigger-Neutralized Counterfactual Scoring (TNCS), which isolates a trigger's contribution to the target behavior by comparing the scoring model's output on a triggered input with its output on a trigger-neutralized counterfactual, covering classification and generation alike. TNCS attains higher attack success rates than the baselines at very low poisoning ratios, across three CodeXGLUE tasks and five trigger families, without degrading clean-task performance, and its margin grows as the ratio decreases. It is the only method effective on every trigger family, and its selected poison set transfers to mismatched victims.


### [334] [Beyond Copying: Lifelong Smart Contract Security Task Construction via Protocol Mutation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32453)
*Zifan Peng, Peng Huang, Jingyi Zheng, Yingjie Xue, Xuechao Wang, Xinlei He, Xia Hu, Dongrui Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: AI agents can inspect code and run tests to support smart contract security. SlowMist reports about $38.6 billion in losses across the broader blockchain ecosystem. Existing evaluations can reward memorized or copied answers, including attack code recovered from public blockchain transactions. Reconstructing contracts and validating tasks also require costly manual effort. To address these issues, we propose CONSTRAINTDIAL, a framework for lifelong construction of smart contract security tasks via protocol mutation. It automates local contract reconstruction and uses attack execution to select changes to the required calls or inputs. Execution checks guide revisions that make the original attack fail while preserving exploitability, the security objective, and normal functionality. New incidents and audit findings enter the same procedure. With CONSTRAINTDIAL, we construct 3,045 task inputs from 203 public cases, covering detect, exploit, and patch. Compared with reconstruction alone, protocol mutation reduces success with supplied public answers by 10.9 percentage points on average across detect, exploit, and patch. The fraction of tasks solved by copying online answers falls by 9.3 points.


### [335] [Fooling LLM Safety Guards into Judging the Wrong Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31695)
*Utkarsh Kanwat*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Safety guards check content before an LLM application acts on it, and most paste that content into a plain-text prompt. Content that imitates the prompt can append a second task, and the guard answers that one instead. We call this target rebinding. Our explanation is that the guard grades the last complete task in its input, here a harmless decoy, so the harmful request goes through unchanged. In a pre-registered test, an appended forged query and decoy clear all 75 held-out AdvBench requests that Shieldstral-1.0-3B flags. The guard is not persuaded: a directive to ignore the text above clears none of them, and in a follow-up run a harmful decoy stops the attack. On our GuardRebind benchmark, each of five guards has an attack family, chosen per guard, that clears at least 93% of its flagged items. Rebinding explains this best on Shieldstral and gpt-oss-safeguard; on ShieldGemma and Llama Guard 3, generic evasion and trailing text respectively do part of the work, and Qwen3Guard falls mainly to a forgery of its whole task. Training the guard to score content no lower than its most harmful part cuts attack success from 75% to 0.8-1.6% on attack families, decoys and forged queries held out of training. All of these attacks append the forged structure after the request, and we added the held-out-decoy evaluation after a first round of test runs. Clean AUROC falls by at most 0.01, but our test of non-inferiority is inconclusive. A score-guided search still beats this trained guard on 47 of 50 items, usually by placing forged structure before the request. On about half of the items where the search wins, the same search also finds benign text, with no forged structure, that clears the guard.


### [336] [WADE: An LSTM-Based Anomaly Detection Engine for WebAssembly Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31984)
*Ilaria Campestrini, Stefano Zanero, Engin Kirda*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: WebAssembly (Wasm) enables near-native performance for web applications through a portable and sandboxed execution model. However, its limited runtime protection mechanisms (e.g., the lack of stack canaries and address space layout randomization (ASLR)) leave Wasm applications vulnerable to traditional attacks. We present WADE (WebAssembly Anomaly Detection Engine), a system that detects anomalous runtime behaviors in browser-executed WebAssembly applications. WADE monitors function-level execution traces collected under benign conditions and trains a Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) network to learn the normal execution behavior of each application. At runtime, the model flags deviations from these learned patterns as potential attacks. Unlike signature-based approaches, WADE builds application-specific behavioral models, enabling detection of both known and previously unseen attacks without per-vulnerability signatures. We evaluate WADE on a dataset of 67 WebAssembly applications spanning ten vulnerability classes, achieving zero false negatives and a 0.42% false positive rate.


### [337] [TRACE: Data-Free Text Reconstruction Attacks against Approximate Unlearning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31995)
*Mengying Zhang, Derui Wang, Ibrahim Khalil, Bowen Liu, Xiaoyu Xia, Minhui Xue*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) face growing demands for data removal driven by regulatory compliance, copyright concerns, and privacy protection, approximate unlearning has emerged as a practical alternative to expensive full retraining. In practice, pre- and post-unlearning model snapshots are often maintained for auditing and rollback, introducing a previously overlooked side channel. This paper demonstrates that approximate unlearning in LLM can leave residual traces within model snapshots, which may be recoverable and thus enable the privacy breaches that approximate unlearning is intended to prevent. Here, we propose TRACE, a framework that reconstructs unlearned training data given access to pre- and post-unlearning model snapshots, without any data-specific prior knowledge. To achieve this, TRACE identifies a compact set of candidate tokens from embedding-level parameter differences to constrain the search space, and assembles sequences via a contrastive scoring mechanism based on the perplexity gap between the two models. A two-stage exploration-then-completion strategy enables the recovery of multiple distinct samples. Extensive experiments on various datasets, including TOFU, MUSE, and WMDP, across multiple LLMs and six representative unlearning methods, demonstrate that TRACE achieves high-fidelity reconstruction. This paper exposes significant privacy risks in current LLM approximate unlearning deployments and highlights the need for defenses against parameter-difference leakage and appropriate management of model snapshot access.


<div id='cs.NI'></div>

# cs.NI [[Back]](#toc)

### [338] [GLP Networking API Specification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32583)
*Dan Bachar, Ehud Shapiro*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: Multiagent deterministic GLP (madGLP) runs Grassroots Logic Programs (GLP) as agents that communicate solely by asynchronous message passing, and assumes fair delivery: every message sent is eventually delivered. Each agent runs on a person's smartphone, named by the person's public key. A grassroots platform depends on no global resource but the network, so agents must reach one another over Bluetooth Low Energy, on a local network, and across the Internet from behind NAT, via rendezvous servers anyone can run. What madGLP requires of such a network, and what the network requires of the platform, has not been specified. We specify the networking layer beneath madGLP. Anything that can be done in GLP is done in GLP: the layer provides only what GLP cannot reach - the network stack, the location service, application attestation, and cryptographic primitives. Three transport underlays share one interface. Over PAN and LAN, an agent advertises a rotating recognition token derived from its key and time slot, so a peer who knows the key recognises it before contact and nobody else can track it; whether it answers unrecognised contacts is a per-underlay trust level set by GLP. Over WAN, a rendezvous server is a static-address host running the headless layer and a GLP rendezvous agent that coordinates hole punching, relays no payload, and belongs to no social graph. On every underlay, peers establish a mutually authenticated Noise session from Ed25519 keys, then attest, bound to the handshake hash, that each runs the application published under its developer's key, verified against Apple's and Google's roots with no third party contacted. One message protocol, over UDP datagrams and GATT writes, provides the fair, unordered, atomic and authenticated delivery madGLP assumes.


### [339] [Clean-label Poisoning Attack and Defense Game for Federated Learning in Vehicular Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32765)
*Yalin E. Sagduyu, Tugba Erpek, Nathan Stephenson, Mengran Xue*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: This paper studies interactions between clean-label poisoning attack and defense in vehicular networks using federated learning (FL). Basic Safety Message (BSM) classification for anomaly detection is modeled as a distributed learning problem over a vehicular network with dynamic topology. As a critical threat to FL, a feature-space clean-label attack is introduced, wherein benign samples are perturbed toward malicious feature extremes, degrading class separability while preserving labels. To counter this, a feature-consistency relabeling defense is proposed to detect and relabel suspicious samples. A game-theoretic framework is developed to capture attack-defense interactions and characterize equilibrium strategies based on attack effectiveness and cost parameters. Results show that the attack scales effectively with compromised nodes, while the proposed defense keeps attack success low without degrading clean accuracy and shifts equilibrium regions toward attack-averse regimes.


### [340] [Quantum-Classical Link Prediction on Dark Web Hyperlink Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32510)
*Vojtěch Novák, Radovan Rečka, Ondřej Knebl, Aktham Youssef, Ivan Zelinka*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: We analyze HTML content and hyperlinks from an offline Tor crawl containing 383 onion hosts and 2,067 directed cross-host links. The study combines semantic analysis with degree-controlled quantum--classical link prediction. Two directory groups emit 94.8\% of the observed host links. Directed preferential attachment reaches ROC-AUC 0.976 with random non-edges, but falls to $0.550\pm0.014$ under source-conditioned target-degree matching. With disjoint training and test pairs, multimodal logistic regression reaches $0.672\pm0.021$, GAE $0.899\pm0.016$, and VGAE $0.906\pm0.016$. A four-qubit variational quantum classifier (VQC) comparison tests two encodings across 20 disjoint-pair graph splits under exact and 1,024-shot simulation, with classical controls using the same inputs and training examples. Its best exact mean ROC-AUC is $0.549\pm0.023$, compared with $0.551\pm0.016$ for RBF SVC and $0.573\pm0.029$ for a 16-parameter MLP on the same inputs and training examples. These results support predictive signal in combined graph/content features and show why degree control, feature compression, and optimization conditions matter in quantum--classical comparisons.


<div id='cs.DB'></div>

# cs.DB [[Back]](#toc)

### [341] [Scaling Graph Query Processing: Focused Exploration on Partitions Using Load Balancing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32015)
*Harshit Modi, Abhishek Santra, Sharma Chakravarthy*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: The focus of this paper is on query processing and not search (approximate or exact). The goal is to find all or k answers and the expressiveness of a query (as opposed to a search expression which is typically simpler) is considered important. Although SQL query processing has matured over the last several decades, query processing over graph data has not received the same attention. Only recently, systems, such as Neo4J, and others have started using graphs for data representation on a large scale. Their approaches and algorithms remain proprietary. In this paper, we address the problem of answering graph queries and their efficient processing on arbitrary-sized graphs. We propose the partitioning of graphs and the use of the distributed paradigm to achieve efficiency and scalability. Our approach takes a subgraph as query input including wild cards and comparison operations. Our partitioned approach leverages focused exploration and distributed processing to achieve the desired parallelism and improve the response time. We also present heuristics to reduce the number of partitions loaded for query processing. We present our partitioning algorithm, distributed processing architecture used, and load balancing for computing query answers. We establish the correctness of the approach in the presence of partitions, compare heuristics for load balancing, and extensive experimental results over large synthetic and real-world graph data sets.


### [342] [MetaDB: Putting File-System Structure Back into Distributed Databases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32247)
*Zebin Ren, Biao Cao, Kang Chen, Zhihui Yin, Qiushi Chen, Jielong Jian, Jiwei Xiong, Xinxing Wang, Yan Xing, Liguo Duan, Ran Zheng, Xianjun Meng, Zhenyu Hou*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: Storage systems in production increasingly rely on distributed databases for metadata management, yet our experience at Baidu shows that treating a general-purpose database as a black box leaves substantial performance and efficiency untapped. File-system metadata exhibits directory-tree locality, differentiated consistency requirements, and clustered deletions that general-purpose databases largely ignore. We thus propose MetaDB, a metadata-aware distributed transactional database that serves as the shared metadata layer for Baidu's large-scale storage systems. MetaDB addresses three key mismatches between general-purpose databases and metadata workloads: Data Model Mismatch, Transaction Model Mismatch, and Storage Implementation Mismatch. To bridge these gaps, MetaDB combines (1) tree-topology-induced sharding and execution, (2) semantic-aware off-critical-path processing, and (3) a lifecycle-aware storage engine with in-memory MVCC and locality-aware compaction. MetaDB achieves 2.2x-4.1x the throughput of our previous general-purpose database and halves the number of metadata machines. Deployed in production at Baidu for over five years, MetaDB unifies metadata management across the company's storage fleet and scales to over 1,000 metadata nodes, tens of trillions of metadata records, and exabyte-scale storage in a single cluster.


### [343] [Tile-Based Decompression in Compiled GPU Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32695)
*Antonio Boffa, Ananya Gupta, Hamish Nicholson, Anastasia Ailamaki*

Main category: cs.DB

Abstract: Modern GPUs reach record analytical performance only when a query's working set fits within its GPU memory budget, and that budget is under growing pressure: GPUs are partitioned and shared, working sets are outgrowing high-bandwidth memory (HBM), and HBM is a fixed on-device resource whose per-dollar capacity has stagnated across generations. Lightweight compression can relieve this pressure by shrinking both footprint and bandwidth demand, but existing GPU compressed-execution techniques either bind decompression to a specific execution model or materialize decompressed columns in HBM before query execution. We present a compression subsystem for compiled GPU query engines that treats decompression as a first-class physical operator. Compression-specific logic is isolated behind a narrow backend interface, so new GPU-oriented encodings can be added without rewriting downstream operators. We implement the design in Proteus and study where decompressed values should be materialized in the GPU memory hierarchy. Our compiler-integrated shared-memory strategy fuses cooperative tile decompression with tuple-at-a-time query execution inside a single generated GPU kernel, avoiding decompressed-column materialization in HBM. Compared with HeavyDB and Sirius on GPU-resident data, fused on-chip decompression achieves competitive query times while reducing the resident memory footprint by up to 4x and memory-bandwidth consumption by up to 6x.


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [344] [Solving and Learning Navier-Stokes Dynamics with the Neural Basis Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32167)
*Yuhe Wang, Zhu Wang, Min Wang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

Abstract: Physics-informed machine learning has become a major direction in predictive modeling of fluid dynamics, combining governing physics with learned representations. Yet prevailing formulations neither guarantee that small PDE-residual losses imply small solution errors nor scale effectively with increasing problem size. Here we present the neural basis method (NBM) as a new approach for Navier--Stokes dynamics. NBM is built on a ``representation-then-projection'' principle: fixed neural bases define the representation space, while projection of the governing equations determines the expansion coefficients. The same bases define common coordinates for the parametric solution manifold, recasting operator learning as a parameter-to-coefficient mapping and enabling scalability by removing explicit spatial dependence. For incompressible flow, NBM yields a velocity-only formulation whose residual provides an a posteriori velocity-error estimate, establishing a certified, data-free training objective for coefficient learning. Theoretical analysis and numerical experiments demonstrate the accuracy, efficiency, generalization, and scalability of NBM, with substantial improvements over baselines.


### [345] [Let's Do Turbulence Right: In Space and Time!](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32601)
*Maximilian Plattner, Fabian Paischer, Johannes Brandstetter*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

Abstract: The three-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations govern fluid turbulence, a chaotic and non-linear physical phenomenon characterized by complex eddies interacting across vast spatial and temporal scales. A numerically accurate modeling of this multi-scale energy cascade requires fully resolving both the fine spatial structures and the continuous temporal evolution of the flow field. In this paper, we leverage massive, time-resolved direct numerical simulations, which exceed 28 TB per run, to assess how numerical choices govern the resolved (3+1)-dimensional physics of turbulent flows. Building on this analysis, we study how neural networks represent the recorded turbulent motion. Our results highlight that spatial and temporal assessments can disagree for both numerical records and learned representations. We therefore call on the machine learning community to treat (3+1)-dimensional turbulence as a four-dimensional phenomenon.
